Spinning S-matrix Bootstrap in 4d Dedication: Dedicated to the memory of André Martin
Aditya Hebbar, Denis Karateev, and João Penedones

Abstract
We review unitarity and crossing constraints on scattering amplitudes for particles with spin in four dimensional quantum field theories. As an application we study two to two scattering of neutral spin 1/2 fermions in detail. Assuming Mandelstam analyticity of its scattering amplitude, we use the numerical S-matrix bootstrap method to estimate various non-perturbative bounds on quartic and cubic (Yukawa) couplings.



1
Introduction

The space of Quantum Field Theories (QFT) is vast and uncharted.
The numerical S-matrix Bootstrap is a nonperturbative approach to explore this space
Paulos:2016fap
;
Paulos:2016but
;
Paulos:2017fhb
;
Doroud:2018szp
;
He:2018uxa
;
Cordova:2018uop
;
Guerrieri:2018uew
;
Homrich:2019cbt
;
EliasMiro:2019kyf
;
Cordova:2019lot
;
Bercini:2019vme
;
Karateev:2019ymz
;
Karateev:2020axc
;
Correia:2020xtr
;
Bose:2020shm
.
This recent approach is a practical implementation of the decades old idea of S-matrix Theory (see for example the book
Eden:1966dnq
).
So far this method has only been applied to scattering amplitudes of scalar particles.
1
1

1

In fact, most recent papers studied two dimensional QFT where there is no spin.

In this work, we develop the formalism to study 2 to 2 scattering amplitudes of particles with spin in four dimensional QFTs.

The main idea of the numerical S-matrix Bootstrap
Paulos:2016but
;
Paulos:2017fhb
is to write a generic analytic and Lorentz invariant ansatz for the scattering amplitude and then impose the constraints from crossing symmetry and unitarity.
In the case of identical scalar particles of mass
m
, the interacting part of the scattering amplitude denoted by
T(s,t,u)
, where
s
,
t
and
u
are the Mandelstam variables, can be written as

T(s,t,u)=\sum_{a}\alpha_{i}\;F_{i}(s,t,u)\,,

(1)

where
\alpha_{i}
are undetermined real coefficients and
F_{i}(s,t,u)
are functions with appropriate analyticity properties.
2
2

2

In practice, we assume maximal analyticity of the scattering amplitude,
i.e.
the only non-analyticities are the ones predicted by Landau diagrams.

Crossing symmetry

T(s,t,u)=T(t,s,u)=T(u,t,s)\,,

(2)

imposes linear constraints on the coefficients
\alpha_{i}
in the ansatz (
1
).
Finally, the unitarity requirement can be cast as the following semidefinite positive condition
3
3

3

This is equivalent to requiring non-negativity of the determinant of the matrix which leads to the unitarity constraint in a more recognizable form
|\mathcal{S}_{\ell}(s)|^{2}\leq 1
.

\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathcal{S}^{*}_{\ell}(s)\\
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}(s)&1\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0,

(3)

where
\ell=0,2,4,\ldots
and
s\geq 4m^{2}
. The partial amplitudes
\mathcal{S}_{\ell}(s)
are defined as the projection of the scattering amplitude onto definite spin via the Legendre Polynomials
P_{\ell}(\cos\theta)
as

\mathcal{S}_{\ell}(s)\equiv 1+i\frac{\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}}{32\pi\sqrt{s}}\times\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta P_{\ell}(\cos\theta)T(s,t(s,\cos\theta),u(s,\cos\theta)).

(4)

The integration variable
\theta
is the scattering angle and its precise relation to the Mandelstam variables is given in (
67
).
By keeping a finite number of terms in the ansatz (
1
) one can numerically explore the space of consistent scattering amplitudes, namely the ones obeying (
2
) and (
3
). In particular, extremizing any physical parameter linearly related to the scattering amplitude translates into a numerical (primal semidefinite) optimization problem.

In the presence of spin, the expressions (
2
) - (
4
) immediately become more complicated, see
Martin:102663
for a review. The goal of section
2
is to setup the formalism which allows to study crossing and unitarity for amplitudes with generic masses and spins. This section is mostly a review of known results in a concise form.

Compared to the scalar case a generic scattering amplitude describing the process
12\rightarrow 34
has the form

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}),

(5)

where
p_{i}
are four-momenta and
\lambda_{i}
are helicities of scattering particles respectively. Given the spins
j_{i}
of four particles, helicities take values from the range
\lambda_{i}=-j_{i},\ldots+j_{i}
with step one. There are thus

N_{4}\equiv(2j_{1}+1)(2j_{2}+1)(2j_{3}+1)(2j_{4}+1)

(6)

components describing the scattering process
12\rightarrow 34
.
4
4

4

When the scattering process is parity and/or time-reversal invariant, when it contains identical and/or massless particles, the counting of independent amplitudes becomes much more complicated. We discuss it in section
2.4
.
We would then like to define
N_{4}
scattering amplitudes which depend only on the Mandelstam variables.
This can be done in two different ways: using the center of mass frame or using tensor structures. We denote these two options respectively by

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)\qquad\text{and}\qquad T_{12\rightarrow 34}^{I}(s,t,u),

(7)

where
I=1,\ldots N_{4}
. The two sets of amplitudes in (
7
) are related by a linear transformation which depends on the Mandelstam variables only. This relation is completely fixed once the basis of tensor structures is chosen.
Both options have their advantages and disadvantages. In the center of mass frame one can derive crossing equations once and for all spins and masses as summarized in section
2.3
(see appendix
E
for details).
5
5

5

Generically, crossing equations relate physical amplitudes to the analytic continuation of other amplitudes beyond their physical domain.
There is no general proof (especially for particles with spin) that this continuation exists and how big is the domain where crossing equations hold. For a review of results for scalar amplitudes, see
Sommer:1970mr
. In this work we do not address this issue and simply assume that all the amplitudes under consideration are maximally analytic.

However, the analyticity properties of the center of mass amplitudes are subtle and one is
forced to deal with the issue of kinematic singularities discussed in section
2.7
.
Analyticity is more straightforward when using tensor structures, however one needs to study crossing case by case due to non-trivial linear relations between covariant tensor structures.
As a result we cannot give it a completely general treatment in section
2
and instead employ it only in the particular example of identical neutral spin
\frac{1}{2}
particles in section
3.4
.
In addition, the construction of general spin tensor structures is discussed to some extent in appendix
H
.

The key element for imposing unitarity constraints are the partial amplitudes (
4
). In the case of generic spin, (
4
) remains valid for center of mass amplitudes, if the Legendre polynomial is replaced by the small Wigner d-matrix given in (
16
) in full generality. This is explained in detail in section
2.5
.
Finally, the unitarity constraints for generic spin are given in section
2.6
.

In section
3
, we specialize to the case of scattering of neutral spin
\frac{1}{2}
massive fermions, also known as Majorana fermions
Majorana:1937vz
. This section should be seen as the simplest application of the formalism given in section
2
. In section
4
we write an ansatz for the scattering of identical Majorana particles in a parity invariant QFT (assuming this is the lightest particle).
We then setup the optimization problem using crossing and unitarity constraints derived in section
3
. Finally, we present our numerical results for the allowed values of the non-perturbative quartic and cubic (Yukawa) couplings defined from the physical scattering amplitude in section
3.3
. The universal bounds for the quartic coupling, given in (
251
), and for the cubic couplings, shown in figures
5
and
6
, are our main numerical results.

Our work opens the ground for many other interesting bootstrap studies in 4 dimensions. For example, photon-photon, pion-nucleon or spinning glueball scattering.
We conclude in section
5
with a discussion of these examples and other open questions for the future. We also include several appendices that fill in the details of the presentation in the main text.

2
Review: spinning
S
-matrix approach

In this paper we study quantum systems invariant under the restricted Poincaré symmetry group. In addition, we consider special situations where parity and/or time-reversal symmetries are also present.
Scattering amplitudes are important physical observables in these systems.
They are constrained by many non-trivial consistency conditions.
The program of deriving and exploiting such theoretical constraints is referred to as the S-matrix bootstrap.

The S-matrix bootstrap program was extensively developed half a century ago. In this section we review its kinematic aspects (the ones fixed by symmetries).
6
6

6

For other sources covering this topic see for example
Weinberg:1995mt
.
Our goal is to provide an updated and easy to use practical summary of the basic ingredients.

2.1
States

We work in Lorentzian metric with the mostly plus signature

\eta_{\mu\nu}=\{-+++\}.

(8)

The unitary irreducible representations of the restricted Poincaré group were classified by Wigner.
7
7

7

For a textbook discussion see for example
Weinberg:1995mt
;
Tung:1985na
.
Here we will work with a particular unitary representation which is positive energy time-like. The basis for such a representation is formed by the states

|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle\,.

(9)

where the 3-momentum
\vec{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the helicity
\lambda=-\ell,-\ell+1,\ldots,+\ell
.
The other labels are fixed within the irreducible representation and serve to specify a given state.
The label
c>0
is a continuous real parameter related to the energy
p^{0}>0
as

c^{2}=-p_{\mu}p^{\mu}=(p^{0})^{2}-(\vec{p}\,)^{2}.

(10)

It is defined as the eigenvalue of the first Casimir of the Poincaré group
c^{2}=-P^{2}
, where
P^{\mu}
are the generators of translations. Instead of
c
one can equivalently label the basis of states (
9
) using the energy
p^{0}
. We will often use both labels throughout the text interchangeably.
The spin label
\ell=0,\,\tfrac{1}{2},\,1,\ldots
is a non-negative integer or half integer related to the eigenvalue of the second Casimir of the Poincaré group
W^{2}=4c^{2}\,\ell(\ell+1)
, where
W^{\mu}
is the Pauli–Lubanski pseudovector.
8
8

8

In our convention
W_{\mu}\equiv\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}M^{\nu\rho}P^{\sigma}
, where
M^{\nu\rho}
are the generators of the Lorentz group.

The helicity label
\lambda
is the projection of the spin vector along
\vec{p}
.
Finally the label
\gamma
stands for any other additional (discrete or continuous) labels which might be required to fully specify the state.
We will see one particular case of their importance in section
2.1.2
.

We will often need to use spherical coordinates in which case the three momentum is parametrized as

\vec{p}=(\boldsymbol{p}\sin\theta\cos\phi,\;\boldsymbol{p}\sin\theta\sin\phi,\;\boldsymbol{p}\cos\theta),\quad\boldsymbol{p}\equiv|\vec{p}|,\quad 0\leq\phi<2\pi,\quad 0\leq\theta\leq\pi,

(11)

The helicity basis states (
9
) with non-zero three momentum
\vec{p}
are defined as follows

|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle=e^{-i\phi J_{3}}e^{-i\theta J_{2}}e^{+i\phi J_{3}}e^{-i\eta K_{3}}|c,\vec{0};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle,

(12)

where
J_{a}
and
K_{a}
with
a=1,2,3
are the generators of spatial rotations and boosts respectively. The rapidity parameter
\eta
is related to the energy and momentum of the states as

\cosh\eta=\frac{p^{0}}{c},\qquad\sinh\eta=\frac{\boldsymbol{p}}{c}.

(13)

Lastly the helicity
{\lambda}
in the zero three momentum state
|c,\vec{0};j,{\lambda};\gamma\rangle
is a projection of the spin
{\ell}
onto the
z
axis. In other words these states are eigenstates of the
J_{3}
generators. Given a generic 3d rotation characterized by the three Euler angles
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
(where
\alpha
and
\gamma
parametrize rotations around the
z
-axis and
\beta
parametrizes rotations around the
y
-axis) we have the following transformation property

R(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)|c,\vec{0};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(\ell)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)|c,\vec{0};{\ell},{\lambda}^{\prime};\gamma\rangle,\quad R(\vec{a})\equiv e^{-i\alpha J_{3}}e^{-i\beta J_{2}}e^{-i\gamma J_{3}}.

(14)

The object
\mathscr{D}^{(\ell)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}
is known as the large Wigner D matrix and reads as

\mathscr{D}^{(\ell)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=e^{-i\alpha\lambda^{\prime}}d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\beta)e^{-i\gamma\lambda}.

(15)

Here the object
d^{({\ell})}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}
is the small Wigner d matrix, its explicit expression reads
9
9

9

Note that Mathematica implements the small Wigner d matrices (
16
) with indices
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
flipped. In other words
d^{({\ell})}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\beta)
is generated by the command
\text{WignerD}[\{{\ell},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\},\beta]
.

\displaystyle d^{({\ell})}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{({\ell}+\lambda)!({\ell}-\lambda)!({\ell}+\lambda^{\prime})!(\ell-\lambda^{\prime})!}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\nu=0}^{2j}(-1)^{\nu}\frac{\left(\cos(\beta/2)\right)^{2j+\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}-2\nu}\left(-\sin(\beta/2)\right)^{\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda+2\nu}}{\nu!(\ell-\lambda^{\prime}-\nu)!({\ell}+\lambda-\nu)!(\nu+\lambda^{\prime}-\lambda)!}.

(16)

In case both helicities
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are equal to zero, the small Wigner d matrix reduces to the usual Legendre polynomial

d^{(\ell)}_{00}(\beta)=P_{\ell}(\cos\beta).

(17)

The Wigner D matrices satisfy orthogonality. They also obey various useful relations, see for example appendix A.2 in
Martin:102663
.

Consider now a generic Poincaré transformation which consists of a translation fixed by four Lie parameters
a^{\mu}
and the Lorentz transformation
\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{\nu}
fixed by six Lie parameters
\rho_{[\mu\nu]}
. It transforms the 4-momentum
p^{\mu}
into
p^{\prime\mu}=\Lambda^{\mu}{}_{\nu}p^{\nu}
. Under the Poincaré transformation
(a,\rho)
the states (
9
) transform according to

U(a,\rho)|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle=e^{ia\cdot p^{\prime}}\times\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(\ell)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)|c,\vec{p}^{\,\prime};{\ell},{\lambda}^{\prime};\gamma\rangle,

(18)

where the three Wigner angles
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
can be expressed in terms of the six Lie parameters
\rho_{[\mu\nu]}
. We provide an example of a Wigner angles computation in appendix
A.4
. For the special case of pure 3d rotations, the Wigner D matrix reduces to a phase and therefore the helicity of the state remains unchanged
10
10

10

Notice that this is compatible with (
14
) because the limit
\vec{p}\to 0
of
|c,\vec{p};j,{\lambda};\gamma\rangle
is only equal to
|c,\vec{0};j,{\lambda};\gamma\rangle
if
\vec{p}
is parallel to the
z
-axis.

R(\vec{a})|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle=e^{-i\lambda\,\xi(\vec{a},\phi,\theta)}|c,\vec{p}\,^{\prime};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle,

(19)

where the phase
\xi
has a complicated dependence on its arguments.

Particles

So far we discussed states and their properties in a generic Poincaré invariant quantum theory.
The related notion of particle does not exist in every QFT. The simplest context where particles are well defined is in free QFTs.
A free theory is described by a set of one particle states (describing freely propagating particles with a given mass and spin). Taking tensor products of these states we form a complete basis of states spanning the Hilbert space of the theory.
In interacting QFTs, particles can still be defined in the asymptotic far past and future if interactions decay sufficiently fast with distance. This is the case for theories with a mass gap and massless theories with soft interactions like Goldstone bosons or photons (in the absence of charged particles in the asymptotic states).
In this paper, we will only consider those theories where particles do exist.
Then, one can define two types of asymptotic states: the
in
states (far past) and the
out
states (far future).
Both the
in
states and the
out
states span the full Hilbert space of the theory.
A natural basis of
in/out
states is given by the tensor product of one particle
in/out
states.

2.1.1
One particle states (1PS)

One particle states (1PS) are special cases of (
9
) where
c
takes only discrete values corresponding to the masses of stable particles in the theory.
For this reason, we will set
c=m
from now on.
If global symmetries are present, the label
\gamma
describes the charge or more generically the representation under the global symmetry group.
For simplicity, in this section we ignore global symmetries, thus the 1PS will not carry any extra labels
\gamma
.

We introduce a shorthand notation for one particle states

|\kappa\rangle\equiv|m,\vec{p};j,\lambda\rangle.

(20)

For indicating spin we use the label
j
instead of
{\ell}
here to ease the visual distinction between the 1PS and generic irreps.
We normalize 1PS as follows

\displaystyle\langle m^{\prime},\vec{p}^{\,\prime};j^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}|m,\vec{p};j,\lambda\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{3}\,2p^{0}\,\delta^{3}(\vec{p}^{\,\prime}-\vec{p})\,\delta_{m^{\prime}m}\delta_{j^{\prime}j}\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}

(21)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\delta(\kappa^{\prime}-\kappa),

where in accordance with (
10
) the energy of the states is

p^{0}=\sqrt{m^{2}+p^{2}}.

(22)

In the second line of (
21
) we have introduced the shorthand notation
\delta(\kappa^{\prime}-\kappa)
for the set of Kronecker and Dirac delta functions.
The transformation rule for a one particle state under a Poincare transformation remains the same as in (
18
).

The case of massless particles should be treated separately since
c\neq 0
in (
9
). Skipping details, the following statement holds: massless particles can be described by the states (
20
) with
m=0
and the range of helicities restricted to only two values
\lambda=-j
and
\lambda=+j
.

Finally we state the transformation properties of 1PS under parity and time-reversal. For further details see appendix
B
. One has

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}|m,\vec{p};j,\lambda\rangle

\displaystyle=\eta(-1)^{j-\lambda}\exp(2i\phi\lambda)|m,-\vec{p};j,-\lambda\rangle,

(23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}|m,\vec{p};j,\lambda\rangle

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(-1)^{2j}\exp(-2i\phi\lambda)|m,-\vec{p};j,\lambda\rangle,

(24)

where
\phi
is the spherical angle defined in (
11
), see also (
34
),
\eta
and
\epsilon
are pure phases.
Their values are yet other quantities characterizing the state. Let us briefly discuss the phase
\eta
called intrinsic parity. One can argue as in section 3.3 of
Weinberg:1995mt
, that one can always define
{\mathcal{P}}
in such a way that
{\mathcal{P}}^{2}=+1
or
{\mathcal{P}}^{2}=-1
. As a result one has

\eta=\pm 1\quad\text{or}\quad\eta=\pm i.

(25)

The imaginary values of intrinsic parities are only possible for fermions, however no such fermions have been discovered so far in the nature.

2.1.2
Two particle states (2PS)

We define the two particle states (2PS) by taking the ordered tensor product of two 1PS

|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle\equiv|m_{1},\vec{p}_{1};j_{1},\lambda_{1}\rangle\otimes|m_{2},\vec{p}_{2};j_{2},\lambda_{2}\rangle.

(26)

The normalization of the 2PS defined above follows from that of the 1PS:

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}|\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{3})\delta(\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{4}).

(27)

Two particle states do not form an irreducible representation of the restricted Poincaré group. However, they can be decomposed into a direct sum of states (
9
) transforming in irreducible representations. This is done by injecting the completeness relation into 2PS

|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle=\int\frac{d^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,\theta(p^{0})\theta(-p^{2})\sum_{\gamma}\sum_{\ell,\lambda}|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle\,\langle c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle,

(28)

where we normalize the states (
9
) as follows
11
11

11

The Kronecker deltas follow from the fact that the states here are simultaneous eigenstates of the two Casimirs (
285
) and the helicity operator (
305
).

\langle c^{\prime},\vec{p}^{\,\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\gamma^{\prime}|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}({p^{\prime}}^{\mu}-p^{\mu})\,\delta_{\ell^{\prime}\ell}\,\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}\,\delta_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma},

(29)

where
p^{\mu}=(p^{0},\,\vec{p})
is the 4-momentum and the symbolic expression
\delta_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma}
will be properly specified when the additional labels
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are defined.
We use the normalization (
29
) for all irreducible Poincaré representations, with the exception of 1PS which are the only states where the label
c
takes particular discrete values.
Looking at the right-hand side of (
28
), we see that the Clebsch-Gordon coefficients of this decomposition (due to translation invariance) obey

\langle c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle\propto(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p^{\mu}-p_{1}^{\mu}-p_{2}^{\mu}).

(30)

This delta function completely removes the integration over
p
in (
28
).
The label
\gamma
is the multiplicity label. In the case of 2PS decomposition the multiplicity label
\gamma
consists of only discrete parameters
12
12

12

This is no longer the case for the decompositions of three or more particle states. In these cases the multiplicity label
\gamma
would also contain continuous parameters associated with the relative momenta of the component particles.
and reads as

\gamma=(m_{1},j_{1},\lambda_{1};\,m_{2},j_{2},\lambda_{2}).

(31)

Thus the label
\gamma
keeps track of which particles and what helicities were used to make the two particle state. In what follows we will almost always drop the explicit mass and spin labels in order to simplify the formulas. However, when dealing with particles of different masses and spins, the mass and spin labels are important.
Finally we choose to normalize the states appearing in (
28
) according to (
29
) with

\delta_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma}=\delta_{m_{1}^{\prime}m_{1}}\delta_{m_{2}^{\prime}m_{2}}\delta_{j_{1}^{\prime}j_{1}}\delta_{j_{2}^{\prime}j_{2}}\times\delta_{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\lambda_{1}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\lambda_{2}}.

(32)

COM two particle states

We can always use Lorentz invariance to go to the frame where the total momentum of the two particles is 0. This frame is called the centre of mass (COM) frame. Therefore, we do not need to know the most general decomposition (
28
), and instead it is enough to focus on the special case of 2PS in the center of mass (COM) frame, namely the states (
26
) obeying the constraint
\vec{p}_{1}=-\vec{p}_{2}
. We give a special label to such two particles states

\displaystyle|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle\equiv|m_{1},+\vec{p};j_{1},\lambda_{1}\rangle\otimes|m_{2},-\vec{p};j_{2},\lambda_{2}\rangle,

(33)

where the three-momenta have the following spherical coordinates
13
13

13

Notice that given the vector
+\vec{p}
in spherical coordinates, the vector opposite to it is defined as
-\vec{p}=(\boldsymbol{p},\,\pi-\theta,\,\pi+\phi)
for
\phi\in[0,\pi]
and
-\vec{p}=(\boldsymbol{p},\,\pi-\theta,\,-\pi+\phi)
for
\phi\in(\pi,2\pi]
. In other words in order to describe the vector
-\vec{p}
in spherical coordinates one needs two different descriptions, one for
\phi\in[0,\pi]
and one for
\phi\in(\pi,2\pi]
. We do not indicate it in the main text, since all the consequent formulas remain uniform in the whole range
\phi\in[0,2\pi]
.
The reason for that is the choice of the helicity basis (
12
) and the fact that
R(\phi,\theta,-\phi)
is
2\pi
periodic, see footnote
42
.
Notice also that there is a special case when
\theta=\pi
. For this particular point, we choose the spherical angles of the first state to be
+\vec{p}=(\boldsymbol{p},\,\theta=\pi,\,\phi=\pi)
, whereas the spherical angles of the second state are
-\vec{p}=(\boldsymbol{p},\,\theta=0,\,\phi=0)
.

+\vec{p}=(\boldsymbol{p},\,\theta,\,\phi),\quad-\vec{p}=(\boldsymbol{p},\,\pi-\theta,\,\pi+\phi),\quad\theta\in[0,\pi],\quad\phi\in[0,2\pi].

(34)

The states (
33
) are normalized according to (
27
). By performing a change of variables we can rewrite the normalization in terms of spherical coordinates as

\langle(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}|(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},0,0);\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\rangle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(0)\times\frac{16\pi^{2}\sqrt{s}}{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}}\frac{\delta(\theta)\delta(\phi)}{\sin\theta}\times\\
\delta_{m_{1}^{\prime}m_{1}}\delta_{m_{2}^{\prime}m_{2}}\delta_{j_{1}^{\prime}j_{1}}\delta_{j_{2}^{\prime}j_{2}}\times\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}},

(35)

where
s=(p_{1}^{0}+p_{2}^{0})^{2}
is the square of the COM energy.
In appendix
A.2.4
, we compute the Clebsch-Gordon coefficients (
30
) for the COM states (
33
).
Here we present only the result which reads as

|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle=\sum_{\ell,\lambda}C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})\,e^{i\phi(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)}d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(\theta)|c,0;{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle,

(36)

where we have

\lambda_{12}\equiv\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2},\quad c=\sqrt{s}=\sqrt{m_{1}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}+\sqrt{m_{2}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{2}},

(37)

with the multiplicity labels
\gamma
given in (
31
) and
d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(\theta)
given by (
16
).
Using the orthogonality of the small Wigner
d
matrix and the exponential function, we can invert equation (
36
) as follows

|c,0;{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle=\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta\,e^{-i\phi(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)}d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(\theta)|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle.

(38)

The coefficient
C_{\ell}
is completely fixed by the consistency requirement that the left-hand side of (
36
) satisfies the normalization condition (
27
) and the state in the right-hand side of (
36
) satisfies the normalization condition (
29
).
For non-identical particles it reads as

\displaystyle C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})^{2}=4\pi\,(2\ell+1)\times\frac{c}{\boldsymbol{p}}.

(39)

Identical particles

The discussion presented above should be slightly modified when the two particle state is composed of identical particles. In the latter case it must satisfy

|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{id}=(-1)^{2j}|\kappa_{2},\kappa_{1}\rangle_{id}.

(40)

We have added the subscript
id
to explicitly indicate that the state describes a system of two identical particles.
In order to incorporate (
40
), we have (instead of simply taking an ordered product) to take either symmetrized (in case of bosons) or anti-symmetrized (in case of fermions) tensor product. Such a state will thus have the following form

|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{id}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(|m,\vec{p}_{1};j,\lambda_{1}\rangle\otimes|m,\vec{p}_{2};j,\lambda_{2}\rangle+(-1)^{2j}|m,\vec{p}_{2};j,\lambda_{2}\rangle\otimes|m,\vec{p}_{1};j,\lambda_{1}\rangle\right).

(41)

The normalization of the state (
41
) follows from (
21
)

{}_{id}\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}|\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}\rangle_{id}=\delta(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{3})\delta(\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{4})+(-1)^{2j}\delta(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{4})\delta(\kappa_{2}-\kappa_{3}).

(42)

As before we need to define the identical 2PS in the center of momentum. Adapting (
33
) to the case of identical particles we get

|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{\text{id}}\equiv\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(|m,+\vec{p};j,\lambda_{1}\rangle\otimes|m,-\vec{p};j,\lambda_{2}\rangle+(-1)^{2j}|m,-\vec{p};j,\lambda_{2}\rangle\otimes|m,\vec{p};j,\lambda_{1}\rangle\right).

(43)

The normalization of the identical COM states (
43
) is fixed by (
42
). It is still given by (
29
) but with

\delta_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}+(-1)^{\ell+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\right).

(44)

We would now like to decompose the identical two particle state (
43
) into irreducible representations

|c,0,{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(|c,0,{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle+(-1)^{\ell+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}|c,0,{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\rangle\right).

(45)

The decomposition of identical 2PS is obtained straightforwardly by applying (
36
) to both terms in the right-hand side of (
43
) which leads to

|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{\text{id}}=\sqrt{2}\sum_{\ell,\lambda}C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})e^{i(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)\phi}d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(\theta)|c,0;{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id},

(46)

where the coefficient
C_{\ell}
is given by (
39
). For the detailed derivation of these equations see appendix
C
.

2.2
S-matrix elements

Given a generic state in the reference frame at
t=0
, an observer in another reference frame in the far past (
t=-\infty
) or far future (
t=+\infty
) will see the same state as a (linear combination of) tensor product of one particle states which we refer to as
in
or
out
asymptotic states respectively. Asymptotic states have a complicated time evolution. One can however establish a formal one to one map between these states and those of some free theory (which evolve trivially with time) by means of a pair of unitary operators
\Omega_{-}
and
\Omega_{+}
called the Møller operators. See section 2.1 in
Karateev:2019ymz
for a recent discussion. In their notation

|\kappa\rangle_{in}=\Omega_{-}|\kappa\rangle_{free},\quad|\kappa\rangle_{out}=\Omega_{+}|\kappa\rangle_{free}.

(47)

Let us now discuss inner products between asymptotic states. Since the Møller operators are isometric,
14
14

14

Recall that an isometric operator
O
on a Hilbert space preserves distances. This implies that
O^{\dagger}O=1
, however the operator need not be surjective and
OO^{\dagger}\neq 1
generically. A surjective isometric operator is unitary.
the inner products of only
in
states or only
out
states are fixed by the normalization conditions (
29
) and (
21
). This means that the only non-trivial matrix elements must include both
in
and
out
states.

Four-particle amplitudes

We start with the most important object for our work

{}_{out}\langle\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{in}={}_{free}\langle\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}|S|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{free},

(48)

where the scattering operator
S
is defined via (
47
) as

S\equiv\Omega_{+}^{\dagger}\Omega_{-}.

(49)

Møller operators are isometric which implies the unitarity of the scattering operator
15
15

15

This is a non-trivial statement, see
taylor2012scattering
for details.

S^{\dagger}S=1.

(50)

Poincaré invariance implies that
16
16

16

See the discussion around (
18
).

U(a,\rho)\,S\,U^{-1}(a,\rho)=S,

(51)

where
U
represents a generic Poincaré transformation in the Hilbert space.
Finally it is convenient to split the scattering operator into the trivial part (identity operator) and the interacting part as

S=1+iT.

(52)

If
T=0
we simply recover the free theory.
The matrix element (
48
) describes scattering of two particles.
Factoring out the overall delta function due to translation invariance we can define the two to two scattering amplitude as

(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}-p_{3}^{\mu}-p_{4}^{\mu})\times S_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\equiv{}_{free}\langle\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}|S|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{free}.

(53)

Equivalently we define the interacting part of the two to two scattering amplitude

(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}-p_{3}^{\mu}-p_{4}^{\mu})\times T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\equiv{}_{free}\langle\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}|T|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{free}.

(54)

Since the (interacting) scattering amplitude is defined via the one particle states, all the 4-momenta are on-shell

p_{i}^{2}=-m_{i}^{2}.

(55)

We study these amplitudes and their properties in depth in section
2.3
. We sometimes drop the subscript
12\rightarrow 34
when it is clear from the context which scattering process we describe.
However, it is necessary to keep this subscript when relating amplitudes describing different processes. Using (
52
) we can relate the scattering amplitude (
53
) with its interacting part (
54
) as

S_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\\
\left[\frac{{}_{free}\langle\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{free}}{(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}-p_{3}^{\mu}-p_{4}^{\mu})}\right]+iT_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}),

(56)

where the first term
[\ldots]
is a schematic expression which can be evaluated straightforwardly.
17
17

17

For example in the COM frame defined in (
65
) using the normalization condition in spherical coordinates (
35
) it is straightforward to write

\left[\frac{{}_{free}\langle\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{free}}{(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}-p_{3}^{\mu}-p_{4}^{\mu})}\right]_{com}=\frac{8\pi\sqrt{s}}{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}}\times\frac{\delta(\theta)\delta(\phi)}{\sin\theta}\delta_{m_{1}m_{3}}\delta_{m_{2}m_{4}}\delta_{j_{1}j_{3}}\delta_{j_{2}j_{4}}\times\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{4}}.

Similarly in case of identical particles with mass
m
and spin
j
using (
466
) we have

\left[\frac{{}_{free}\langle\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{free}}{(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}-p_{3}^{\mu}-p_{4}^{\mu})}\right]_{com}=\frac{32\pi^{2}\sqrt{s}}{\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}}\times\left(\frac{\delta(\theta)\delta(\phi)}{\sin\theta}\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{4}}+(-1)^{2j}\frac{\delta(\pi-\theta)\delta(\phi+\pi)}{\sin(\pi-\theta)}\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{4}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}\right).

(57)

This piece is not a function but a distribution. Due to the relation (
56
), in practice we will never need to discuss the full amplitude
S
and instead we will only need the interacting
T
amplitude.

Partial amplitudes

The second matrix element we need is between the
in
and
out
states in the irreducible representation

{}_{out}\langle c^{\prime},\vec{p}\,^{\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\gamma^{\prime}|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle_{in}={}_{free}\langle c^{\prime},\vec{p}\,^{\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\gamma^{\prime}|S|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle_{free}.

(58)

Again factoring out the overall delta function due to translational invariance we can define the partial amplitude with a definite spin
\ell
as

(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p^{\mu}-{p^{\prime}}^{\mu})\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\times S_{\ell}{}_{\gamma}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(c)={}_{free}\langle c^{\prime},\vec{p}\,^{\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\gamma^{\prime}|S|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle_{free}.

(59)

Equivalently we can define the interacting part of the partial amplitude as

(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p^{\mu}-{p^{\prime}}^{\mu})\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\times T_{\ell}{}_{\gamma}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(c)={}_{free}\langle c^{\prime},\vec{p}\,^{\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\gamma^{\prime}|T|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle_{free}.

(60)

We prove that the left-hand side of (
59
) and (
60
) must contain
\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
factor in appendix
D.1
.
In a generic situation due to (
29
) the two partial amplitudes are simply related as

S_{\ell}{}_{\gamma}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(c)=\delta_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma}+iT_{\ell}{}_{\gamma}^{\gamma^{\prime}}(c).

(61)

In practice we will only need to consider partial amplitudes where the irreps come from the decomposition of COM two particle states (
36
). In that case the additional labels
\gamma
are multiplicities given by (
31
). The Kronecker delta for distinct particles is given by (
32
) and for identical particles by (
44
).
We examine partial amplitudes and their properties in detail in section
2.5
. In addition, we also derive the relation between partial and scattering amplitudes.

2.3
Scattering amplitudes and crossing

In this section we carefully study various aspects of the scattering amplitudes (
53
) and its interacting part (
54
).

We start with the transformation property under the Poincaré group. It directly follows from the transformation property of each state given by (
18
). In the most generic case it reads as

\displaystyle\resizebox{20348790}{}{$\displaystyle{T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(\vec{\omega}_{1})\mathscr{D}^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(\vec{\omega}_{2})\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(\vec{\omega}_{3})\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(\vec{\omega}_{4})T_{\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{4}^{\prime}}(p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}^{\prime},p_{4}^{\prime})}$},

(62)

where
\vec{\omega}_{i}\equiv(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i})
are the Wigner angles for each one particle state defining the (interacting) scattering amplitude.

Let us now introduce the Mandelstam variables which are invariant quantities under Lorentz transformations

s\equiv-(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2},\quad t\equiv-(p_{1}-p_{3})^{2},\quad u\equiv-(p_{1}-p_{4})^{2},\quad s+t+u=\sum_{i=1}^{4}m_{i}^{2}.

(63)

Using these variables one can split the scattering amplitude
(
54
) into parts invariant under Lorentz transformation and parts transforming non-trivially,

T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=\sum_{I=1}^{(2j_{1}+1)\ldots(2j_{4}+1)}T_{I}(s,t,u)\times\mathbb{T}_{I}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}).

(64)

We refer to the quantities
T_{I}(s,t,u)
as the scalar components of the scattering amplitudes and
\mathbb{T}_{I}
are called tensor structures. The latter ensures the correct transformation property of the amplitude as dictated by (
62
). We abuse notation and call both the full amplitude and its scalar components by the same symbol
T
. It should be clear which is which by the presence of indices and arguments. We construct tensor structures explicitly for a particular example in section
3.4.1
. For a general approach see appendix
H
.

Instead of defining tensor structures and scalar components of the amplitude as in (
64
) one can evaluate the full amplitude in a particular frame. The standard choice for this frame is the center of mass (COM) defined as

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\text{com}}=(E_{1},0,0,+\boldsymbol{p}),

(65)

\displaystyle p_{2}^{\text{com}}=(E_{2},0,0,-\boldsymbol{p}),

\displaystyle p_{3}^{\text{com}}=(E_{3},+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta),

\displaystyle p_{4}^{\text{com}}=(E_{4},-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta).

Here the angle
\theta\in[0,\pi]
. All the parameters in (
65
) can be expressed in terms of the Mandelstam variables. In the simplest case where all four particles have the same mass
m
we have

E_{i}=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2},\quad\boldsymbol{p}=\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}=\sqrt{\frac{s}{4}-m^{2}},\quad\sin\theta=\frac{2\sqrt{tu}}{s-4m^{2}},\quad\cos\theta=\frac{t-u}{s-4m^{2}}.

(66)

Instead of using
(s,t,u)
variables to characterize the scattering process one can also use
(s,\theta)
by using the relations

t=-\frac{s-4m^{2}}{2}(1-\cos\theta),\quad u=-\frac{s-4m^{2}}{2}(1+\cos\theta).

(67)

From these expressions it is clear that the physical range of the Mandelstam variables is

s\geq 4m^{2},\quad t\in[4m^{2}-s,\,0],\quad u\in[4m^{2}-s,\,0].

(68)

For the definition of the center of mass frame for the most generic case see appendix
D.3
.

Using the COM frame we can define the scalar components of the interacting scattering amplitude in either of the two equivalent ways
18
18

18

In order to see the equivalence of two definitions simply compare (
54
) evaluated in the COM frame (
65
) with (
33
).

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\equiv T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p^{\text{com}}_{1},p^{\text{com}}_{2},p^{\text{com}}_{3},p^{\text{com}}_{4}),

(69)

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(0)

\displaystyle\equiv{}_{free}\langle(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\theta,0);\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}|T|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{free},

As in (
64
) we abuse notation and call the full (interacting) amplitude in a generic frame and its particular form in the COM frame by the same symbol. The difference should always be clear from the arguments.
Given the interacting scattering amplitude in the COM frame one can unambiguously obtain the interacting scattering amplitude in a generic frame by using (
62
). As an example, let us apply a rotation by an angle
\phi
around the z axis to (
69
). One gets the following relation

e^{i(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})\phi}T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(0)=\\
{}_{free}\langle(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}|T|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{free}

(70)

This result will be useful when we compute the partial wave decomposition of the scattering amplitude in section
2.5
.

We note that the scalar components of the scattering amplitude defined in (
64
) and the COM frame amplitude defined in (
69
) are simply related by a linear transformation which depends only on
s
,
t
and
u
variables. This relation can be found by evaluating (
64
) in the center of mass frame and comparing with (
69
). We will see an explicit example of this in section
3.4.1
.

Parity and time-reversal

Let us now discuss additional constraints which appear if the system is parity or time-reversal invariant.
In terms of the
S
operator (and hence also the
T
operator), the following must hold

{\mathcal{P}}S{\mathcal{P}}^{\dagger}=S,\quad{\mathcal{T}}S{\mathcal{T}}^{\dagger}=S^{\dagger}.

(71)

At the level of COM amplitudes these translate into the following conditions

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\eta_{3}^{*}\eta_{4}^{*}(-1)^{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}}(-1)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2}}^{-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u),

(72)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{1}^{*}\varepsilon_{2}^{*}\varepsilon_{3}\varepsilon_{4}(-1)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}T_{34\rightarrow 12}{}_{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s,t,u).

(73)

We derive them in appendix
B
.

Crossing

Our goal now is to formulate crossing equations. The case of particles with generic spin was first addressed in
Trueman:1964zza
. It was further discussed in
Martin:102663
;
Hara:1970gc
;
Hara:1971kj
. For a recent discussion see also
deRham:2017zjm
. All the results presented below are carefully derived in appendix
E
.

Consider the scattering process of four particles. We denote it schematically by

12\rightarrow 34.

(74)

Each particle is characterized by its mass, spin, helicity and 3-momentum, for instance
1=(m_{1},j_{1},\lambda_{1};\vec{p}_{1})
.
There exist five other related process

\displaystyle\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1},\qquad\qquad\bar{3}2\rightarrow\bar{1}4,

(75)

\displaystyle 1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4,\qquad\qquad 1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}.

together with
\bar{3}\bar{4}\rightarrow\bar{1}\bar{2}
.
Here if particle
i
has a charge (or more generally transforms in some representation of a global group) then particle
\bar{i}
has the opposite charge (transforms in the conjugate representation). In other words
\bar{i}
is the antiparticle of particle
i
. The scattering process (
74
) is described by the following interacting part of the amplitude

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}),

(76)

whereas the scattering processes in (
75
) are described by

\displaystyle T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},p_{1}),\qquad T_{\bar{3}2\rightarrow\bar{1}4}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4}}(p_{3},p_{2},p_{1},p_{4}),

(77)

\displaystyle T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{3},p_{2},p_{4}),\qquad T_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2}}(p_{1},p_{4},p_{3},p_{2}).

Here all 4-momenta have positive energies
p_{i}^{0}>0
and are on-shell (
55
).

Under the assumption that the amplitudes in (
76
) and (
77
) can be analytically continued in
p_{i}
and defined in some common domain of
p_{i}
values, one can write a set of crossing equations

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{14}T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{}_{-\lambda_{4},+\lambda_{2}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1}),

(78)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{23}T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},+\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4}).

(79)

which are referred to as the
s-t
crossing equations and

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{13}T_{\bar{3}2\rightarrow\bar{1}4}{}_{-\lambda_{3},+\lambda_{2}}^{-\lambda_{1},+\lambda_{4}}(-p_{3},p_{2},-p_{1},p_{4}),

(80)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{24}T_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{4}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{2}}(p_{1},-p_{4},p_{3},-p_{2}),

(81)

which are referred to as the
s-u
crossing equations. Here
\epsilon_{14}
,
\epsilon_{23}
,
\epsilon_{13}
and
\epsilon_{24}
are some phases which can be fixed in principle using the LSZ procedure. Finally the amplitude for the process
\bar{3}\bar{4}\rightarrow\bar{1}\bar{2}
can be related to that of the
12\rightarrow 34
process by using (
80
) and (
81
) one after the other. There are two distinct ways for the analytic continuation of amplitudes discussed in section
E.1
. In writing (
78
) - (
81
) we have made a particular choice, more precisely the one given by (
529
).

Let us now focus on the 23- and 24-crossing equations given by (
79
) and (
81
) respectively and evaluate them in the standard COM (
65
). We can then use the definition of the center of mass amplitude (
69
) in the left-hand side of (
79
) and (
81
) but not in the right-hand side. The right-hand side is not in the center of mass frame of particles 13 and 14 respectively. In order to bring them to this frame we need to perform a Lorentz transformation. The 23-crossing equation then reads

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{23}^{\prime}\,\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}e^{i\pi(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}+\lambda_{4}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\times d^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(\alpha_{1})d^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(\alpha_{2})d^{(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(\alpha_{3})d^{(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(\alpha_{4})T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{3}^{\prime}}^{\lambda_{2}^{\prime},\lambda_{4}^{\prime}}(t,s,u),

(82)

where the angles
\alpha_{i}
in the equal mass case are given by

\displaystyle+\cos\alpha_{1}=-\cos\alpha_{2}=-\cos\alpha_{3}=+\cos\alpha_{4}

\displaystyle=+\frac{st}{\sqrt{s(s-4m^{2})}\sqrt{t(t-4m^{2})}},

(83)

\displaystyle+\sin\alpha_{1}=-\sin\alpha_{2}=+\sin\alpha_{3}=-\sin\alpha_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2m\,\sqrt{stu}}{\sqrt{s(s-4m^{2})}\sqrt{t(t-4m^{2})}}.

For the most general case see (
595
) and (
596
).
Similarly the
24
-crossing reads

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{24}^{\prime}\,\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}e^{i\pi(\lambda_{1}^{\prime}+\lambda_{3}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\times d^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(\beta_{1})d^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(\beta_{2})d^{(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(\beta_{3})d^{(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(\beta_{4})T_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{4}^{\prime}}^{\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}(u,t,s),

(84)

where the angles
\beta_{i}
in the equal mass case are given by

\displaystyle+\cos\beta_{1}=-\cos\beta_{2}=+\cos\beta_{3}=-\cos\beta_{4}

\displaystyle=+\frac{su}{\sqrt{s(s-4m^{2})}\sqrt{u(u-4m^{2})}},

(85)

\displaystyle+\sin\beta_{1}=-\sin\beta_{2}=-\sin\beta_{3}=+\sin\beta_{4}

\displaystyle=+\frac{2m\,\sqrt{stu}}{\sqrt{s(s-4m^{2})}\sqrt{u(u-4m^{2})}}.

For the most general case see (
610
) and (
611
).
Notice that small Wigner d-matrices are
4\pi
periodic. As a result the angles
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
are not completely fixed by the equations (
83
) and (
85
) since sines and cosines are
2\pi
periodic. Different choices of
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
in the crossing equations satisfying (
83
) and (
85
) lead to different additional phases which we combine with
\epsilon_{23}
and
\epsilon_{24}
introduced in (
79
) and (
81
) and denote by
\epsilon_{23}^{\prime}
and
\epsilon_{24}^{\prime}
. For each scattering process these phases can be computed at the leading order in perturbation theory and since the phases are purely kinematic in nature, the result can then be used non-perturbatively.
We also notice that the form of crossing equations in a general frame, (
79
) and (
81
), depends on the choice of the analytic continuation, however both analytic continuations lead to the same expressions in the center of mass frame (
82
) and (
84
).

It is interesting to consider the case when all four particles are massless. Assuming the physical domain (
68
) of the Mandelstam variables for the process
12\rightarrow 34
in the limit
m\rightarrow 0
the expressions (
83
) and (
85
) lead to

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}=\pi,\quad\alpha_{2}=0,\quad\alpha_{3}=0,\quad\alpha_{4}=\pi,

(86)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}=\pi,\quad\beta_{2}=0,\quad\beta_{3}=\pi,\quad\beta_{4}=0.

(87)

Using the following properties of the small Wigner d-matrices

d^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(0)=\delta_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda},\qquad d^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\pi)=(-1)^{j-\lambda}\delta_{\lambda^{\prime},\,-\lambda}

(88)

we get the following crossing equations

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{23}^{\prime\prime}T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{\;}{}_{-\lambda_{1},+\lambda_{3}}^{+\lambda_{2},-\lambda_{4}}(t,s,u),

(89)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{24}^{\prime\prime}T_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{\;}{}_{-\lambda_{1},+\lambda_{4}}^{-\lambda_{3},+\lambda_{2}}(u,t,s),

(90)

where
\epsilon_{23}^{\prime\prime}
and
\epsilon_{24}^{\prime\prime}
are some new phases.

Neutral identical particles

In some practical applications one is required to study scattering processes of identical neutral particles with mass
m
and spin
j
. We discuss this case in great detail in appendix
C
. Here we state only the most important results.

We define the scattering amplitude of identical neutral particles in a generic and in the center of mass frames as

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p_{1}^{\mu}+p_{2}^{\mu}-p_{3}^{\mu}-p_{4}^{\mu})\equiv{}_{id\;free}\langle\kappa_{3},\kappa_{4}|T|\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle_{id\;free},

(91)

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(0)\equiv{}_{id\;free}\langle(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\theta,0);\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}|T|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id\;free}.

In comparison with (
53
) we drop the subscript
12\rightarrow 34
since it does not carry any useful information anymore.
The two particle states formed from identical particles were defined in (
41
). According to (
40
) they are (anti)symmetric under the exchange of particles 12 and 34. Due to this condition the following equations hold

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}T_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,u,t),

(92)

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{3}}(s,u,t).

(93)

See appendix
C.3
for details.
Since the variables
t
and
u
are flipped in the left- and right-hand side we refer to them as the t-u crossing equations. Applying them twice we get the following kinematic constraint

T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)=T_{+\lambda_{2},+\lambda_{1}}^{+\lambda_{4},+\lambda_{3}}(s,t,u).

(94)

In the case of distinct particles the crossing equations (
82
) and (
84
) establish relations between different amplitudes. When particles are identical there is only a single amplitude (
91
) and the crossing equations become constraints on this single amplitude.

One can also combine together (
80
), (
81
) and (
78
), (
79
) to obtain

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\,T_{-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{4}}^{-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2}}(-p_{3},-p_{4},-p_{1},-p_{2}),

(95)

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\,T_{-\lambda_{4},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},-\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},-p_{3},-p_{2},-p_{1}).

(96)

Using the techniques of appendix
E.4
one can bring both sides of these two equations to the center of mass frame and show that

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\,T_{-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{4}}^{-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2}}(s,t,u),

(97)

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\,T_{-\lambda_{4},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},-\lambda_{1}}(s,t,u).

(98)

We refer to the conditions (
94
), (
97
) and (
98
) as the kinematic constraints associated to the simultaneous permutation of particles (12)(34), (13)(24) and (14)(23) respectively.

As presented, the transition from (
95
), (
96
) to (
97
), (
98
) is very difficult. Strictly speaking the equations (
97
) and (
98
) might have some overall helicity independent phase which we have little control over. There is a much simpler way of deriving (
97
) however. As discussed in appendix
B.3
one can use the CPT theorem to obtain (
97
). The constraint (
98
) follows from combining (
97
) with (
94
). This discussion indicates (but not proves) that CPT transformation is equivalent to using crossing twice.

2.4
Counting scattering amplitudes

It is useful to count kinematically independent amplitudes in various cases. Looking at the definition of the center of mass amplitude (
69
) it is obvious that the number of all possible amplitudes
N_{4}
for four different massive particles is

N_{4}=(2j_{1}+1)(2j_{2}+1)(2j_{3}+1)(2j_{4}+1),

(99)

since there are no restrictions on the helicity values. Using (
72
) we can further split the amplitudes into parity even and parity odd ones. This is done by taking appropriate linear combinations of

T{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad T{}_{-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2}}^{-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{4}}.

(100)

Since the scattering amplitude must always contain an even number of fermions and due to (
25
) and the comment below, the product of intrinsic parities

\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\eta_{3}^{*}\eta_{4}^{*}

(101)

entering (
72
) is either
+1
or
-1
19
19

19

Parity invariance implies that
\eta_{1}\eta_{2}=\pm\eta_{3}\eta_{4}
.
. Let us assume for concreteness that the product in (
101
) is
+1
. (In the other case when (
101
) is
-1
the role of parity even and odd amplitudes constructed from (
100
) simply flips.)
Having constructed the appropriate linear combination from (
100
) the counting follows straightforwardly. In the case when there are two or four fermions we have the same number of parity even
N_{4}^{+}
and parity odd amplitudes
N_{4}^{-}
which read

N_{4}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(2j_{1}+1)(2j_{2}+1)(2j_{3}+1)(2j_{4}+1).

(102)

In case all particles are bosons we get

N_{4}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}\left((2j_{1}+1)(2j_{2}+1)(2j_{3}+1)(2j_{4}+1)\pm 1\right).

(103)

The difference between (
102
) and (
102
) arises due to the fact that when all particles are bosons there is always a parity even amplitude with all the zero helicities
T{}_{00}^{00}
. The latter is no longer true in the presence of fermions. Clearly, the following is obeyed

N_{4}=N_{4}^{+}+N_{4}^{-}.

(104)

If we impose parity as symmetry of our system only parity even amplitudes will survive.

It is more difficult to perform general counting when particles are identical since we need to take into account the relations (
94
) - (
98
). However it easy to do for any particular case of interest by forming a linear system of all the constraints (due presence of parity, identical and massless particles), solving it and counting the number of independent amplitudes. For example in the case of identical massive Majorana particles and identical massive spin one particles we have

identical Majorana fermions:

\displaystyle N_{4}^{+}=5,\quad\;\;N_{4}^{-}=2,

(105)

identical spin one bosons:

\displaystyle N_{4}^{+}=17,\quad N_{4}^{-}=10.

(106)

Time-reversal does not further reduce these numbers. This can be intuitively understood by noticing that P implies T invariance for neutral particles due to the CPT symmetry.

In case a particle with spin
j
is massless its helicity can only take two values
+j
and
-j
. As a result if all four particles in the scattering process are massless and carry a non-zero spin we always have, independently of the precise values of spin,

\text{four different massless particles:}\quad N_{4}=2^{4}=16.

(107)

In the case when all particles are identical, massless and carry a non-zero spin we have

\text{identical massless particles:}\quad N_{4}^{+}=5,\quad N_{4}^{-}=2.

(108)

It was proposed in section 6 of
Costa:2011mg
that the number of scattering amplitudes in
d
dimensions should be equal to the number of tensor structures of four-point functions in
d-1
conformal field theories, where massless particles correspond to conserved operators. This correspondence got an explanation in
Kravchuk:2016qvl
where it was noted that the conformal frame analysis of four point function is equivalent group theoretically to the center of mass analysis of scattering amplitudes. When parity is involved or particles are identical or massless the matching of CFT and amplitude counting is more difficult to confirm. Here we explicitly verify this correspondence on some particular examples. For instance (
102
) and (
103
) are in a perfect agreement with the formulas (4.47) and (4.49) in
Kravchuk:2016qvl
, results
(
105
) and (
106
) match (2.40) and (4.58) in
Kravchuk:2016qvl
, finally the very special case (
108
) matches (3.24) in
Kravchuk:2016qvl
.
20
20

20

The number of parity even conserved tensor structures in conformal field theories was first computed in
Dymarsky:2013wla
, see table 1. In
d=3
it is 5 as expected.

2.5
Partial amplitudes

As we explained in section
2.1.2
, the two particle states are in a reducible representation of the Poincaré group and can be expressed as a direct sum of Poincaré irreps according to (
28
) or (
36
) (in the special case of COM states). This leads to a decomposition (often referred to as the partial wave decomposition) of scattering amplitudes into partial amplitudes.

We start from the definition of the center of mass amplitude (
70
) and decompose the two particle states there according to (
36
). As a result we get

e^{i(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4})\phi}T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(0)=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime},\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}C_{\ell^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime})C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})\\
e^{-i(\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}-\lambda)\phi}d^{(\ell^{\prime})}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{34}}(\theta)\,d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(0)\langle c,0,{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}|T|c,0,{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle,

(109)

where we have defined

\lambda_{12}\equiv\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2},\quad\lambda_{34}\equiv\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}.

(110)

The coefficient
C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})
was computed in (
39
). Using it we can write

C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime})C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})=4\pi(2\ell+1)\frac{\sqrt{s}}{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}}.

(111)

Due to the standard property of the small Wigner d-matrix

d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(0)=\delta_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}

(112)

the dependence on the azimuthal angle
\phi
in (
109
) cancels out on both sides and we obtain

T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(0)=\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime}}C_{\ell^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime})C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})d^{(\ell^{\prime})}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{34}}(\theta)\langle c,0,{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}|T|c,0,{\ell},\lambda_{12};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle.

Using the definition of the partial amplitudes (
60
) the above can be written in its final form

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell}C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime})C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(\theta)T_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s).

(113)

By using orthogonality of the small Wigner d matrix the decomposition (
113
) can be inverted and leads to

\displaystyle T_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\ell+1}{2C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime})C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})}\times\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(\theta)T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u).

(114)

Note that
t
and
u
are functions of
s
and
\theta
. In the equal mass case one has for instance (
67
).

Analogously to (
114
) one can also write the decomposition of the full amplitude

\displaystyle S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\ell+1}{2C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime})C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})}\times\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\phi}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(\theta)S_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u).

(115)

We need to introduce the integration over the azimuthal angle
\phi
because the disconnected part of the scattering amplitude depends on it, see (
56
) and footnote
17
.
A simple relation between (
114
) and (
115
) partial amplitudes follows from (
61
) and (
32
). It reads
21
21

21

Here we have used a simple fact that

\delta_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}}\delta_{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{l}}\delta_{\lambda_{ij}\lambda_{kl}}=\delta_{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{k}}\delta_{\lambda_{j}\lambda_{l}}.

S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)=\delta_{m_{1}m_{3}}\delta_{m_{2}m_{4}}\delta_{j_{1}j_{3}}\delta_{j_{2}j_{4}}\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{4}}+iT_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s).

(116)

As a consistency check one can obtain this relation in a different way. One can plug (
56
) evaluated in the COM frame together with the very first equation in the footnote
17
into (
115
). The delta functions cancel all the integrals in (
115
) and we simply arrive at (
116
).

Identical particles

In case either the incoming particles or the outgoing particles are identical we also have relations between the partial amplitudes due to (
45
). If the incoming particles 1 and 2 are identical we get

S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)=(-1)^{\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}-\ell}S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}}(s).

(117)

Similarly if the outgoing particles 3 and 4 are identical we get

S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)=(-1)^{\lambda_{4}-\lambda_{3}-\ell}S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{3}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s).

(118)

For the case of all four particles being identical, we get
\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}=\sqrt{s/4-m^{2}}
due to (
66
), and thus

\displaystyle S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}}{32\pi\sqrt{s}}\times\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\phi}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(\theta)S_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u),

(119)

\displaystyle T_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}}{32\pi\sqrt{s}}\times\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda_{12}\lambda_{34}}(\theta)T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u).

(120)

Notice that we have used here (
46
) which contains an additional
\sqrt{2}
factor compared to a non-identical particle case.
Analogously to (
116
) there is a simple relation between the
S
and
T
partial amplitudes that follows from (
61
) and (
44
). It reads
22
22

22

See footnote
21
.

S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{4}}+(-1)^{\ell-\lambda_{34}}\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{4}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}}\right)+iT_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s).

(121)

This result can also be obtained by plugging (
56
) evaluated in the COM frame together with the second equation in the footnote
17
into (
119
). We also notice that in the case of identical scalar particles we recover the standard result, see for example equation (10) in
Paulos:2017fhb
.

Parity and time reversal

As usual parity and time reversal invariance lead to additional constraints. The parity constraint follows directly from (
418
) and reads

S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)=\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\eta_{3}^{*}\eta_{4}^{*}(-1)^{j_{1}-j_{2}+j_{3}-j_{4}-2\ell}S_{\ell}{}^{-\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{4}}_{-\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{2}}(s).

(122)

Similarly the time reversal constraint follows from (
443
) and reads

S_{12\to 34\;\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)=\varepsilon_{1}^{*}\varepsilon_{2}^{*}\varepsilon_{3}\varepsilon_{4}S_{34\to 12\;\ell}{}^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}_{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s).

(123)

Note that we reintroduced the subscripts
12\rightarrow 34
and
34\rightarrow 12
since Time Reversal is a relation between these two distinct processes.

2.6
Unitarity

Unitarity of a quantum theory implies that the norm of any state must be non-negative,
i.e.

\forall

|\psi\rangle
one has
\langle\psi|\psi\rangle\geq 0
. Now suppose we are given some set of
N
states
|\psi_{a}\rangle
with
a=1,\ldots,N
. Unitarity then requires that the
N\times N
hermitian matrix with components
\langle\psi_{a}|\psi_{b}\rangle
is positive semi-definite, namely
\left(\langle\psi_{a}|\psi_{b}\rangle\right)\succeq 0
. This formulation allows us to impose unitarity constraints on the partial amplitudes straightforwardly.

We start by considering the incoming two particles state (formed from particles 1 and 2) together with the outgoing two particle state (formed from particles 3 and 4). We decompose each of these states into irreducible representations according to (
28
).
We then have the following
N=N_{\text{in}}+N_{\text{out}}
independent states transforming in the spin
\ell
irreducible representation of the Poincaré group

\displaystyle|1\rangle_{\text{in}}\equiv|c,\vec{p},{\ell},{\lambda};j_{1},j_{2}\rangle_{\text{in}},\quad

\displaystyle|1\rangle_{\text{out}}\equiv|c,\vec{p},{\ell},{\lambda};j_{3},j_{4}\rangle_{\text{out}},

\displaystyle|2\rangle_{\text{in}}\equiv|c,\vec{p},{\ell},{\lambda};j_{1},j_{2}-1\rangle_{\text{in}},\quad

\displaystyle|2\rangle_{\text{out}}\equiv|c,\vec{p},{\ell},{\lambda};j_{3},j_{4}-1\rangle_{\text{out}},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\vdots

\displaystyle|N_{\rm in}\rangle_{\text{in}}\equiv|c,\vec{p},{\ell},{\lambda};-j_{1},-j_{2}\rangle_{\text{in}},\quad

\displaystyle|N_{\rm out}\rangle_{\text{out}}\equiv|c,\vec{p},{\ell},{\lambda};-j_{3},-j_{4}\rangle_{\text{out}},

(124)

where the number of incoming and outgoing irreducible states is

N_{\text{in}}\equiv(2j_{1}+1)(2j_{2}+1),\qquad N_{\text{out}}\equiv(2j_{3}+1)(2j_{4}+1).

(125)

Thus for each spin
\ell
we can construct the following hermitian
N\times N
matrix

\mathcal{H}_{\ell}(s)\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p^{\prime}-p)\delta_{\ell^{\prime}\ell}\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}=\begin{pmatrix}{}_{\text{in}}\langle a^{\prime}|b\rangle_{\text{in}}&{}_{\text{in}}\langle a^{\prime}|b\rangle_{\text{out}}\\
{}_{\text{out}}\langle a^{\prime}|b\rangle_{\text{in}}&{}_{\text{out}}\langle a^{\prime}|b\rangle_{\text{out}},\end{pmatrix}

(126)

where
s=c^{2}
is the square of the COM frame total energy and the primes indicate that the conjugated states to (
2.6
) have all the labels
c
,
p
,
\ell
and
\lambda
primed. In defining the matrix
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}(s)
we have also explicitly factored out the overall delta function appearing due to translation invariance. According to the discussion above, unitarity then implies that

\mathcal{H}_{\ell}(s)\succeq 0,\qquad\forall\ell\quad\text{ and}\quad s\geq\max(m_{1}+m_{2},m_{3}+m_{4})^{2}.

(127)

Let us now discuss the components of the matrix (
126
). Since the Møller operators introduced in section
2.2
to define the incoming and outgoing states are isometric, the elements
{}_{\text{in}}\langle a^{\prime}|b\rangle_{\text{in}}
and
{}_{\text{out}}\langle a^{\prime}|b\rangle_{\text{out}}
are simply fixed by the normalization condition (
29
)

\displaystyle{}_{\text{in}}\langle c_{1}^{\prime},\vec{p}^{\,\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle|c_{1},\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}({p^{\prime}}^{\mu}-p^{\mu})\,\delta_{\ell^{\prime}\ell}\,\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}\,\delta_{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}\lambda_{1}}\,\delta_{\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\lambda_{2}},

(128)

\displaystyle{}_{\text{out}}\langle c_{1}^{\prime},\vec{p}^{\,\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{4}^{\prime}

\displaystyle|c_{1},\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}\rangle_{\text{out}}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}({p^{\prime}}^{\mu}-p^{\mu})\,\delta_{\ell^{\prime}\ell}\,\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}\,\delta_{\lambda_{3}^{\prime}\lambda_{3}}\,\delta_{\lambda_{4}^{\prime}\lambda_{4}}.

(129)

On the other hand, the matrix elements
{}_{\text{out}}\langle a^{\prime}|b\rangle_{\text{in}}
and
{}_{\text{in}}\langle a^{\prime}|b\rangle_{\text{out}}
form partial amplitudes according to (
59
)

\displaystyle{}_{\text{out}}\langle c_{1}^{\prime},\vec{p}\,^{\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}

\displaystyle|c_{1},\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p^{\mu}-{p^{\prime}}^{\mu})\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\,S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s),

(130)

\displaystyle{}_{\text{in}}\langle c_{1}^{\prime},\vec{p}^{\prime};{\ell}^{\prime},{\lambda}^{\prime};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}

\displaystyle|c_{1},\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}\rangle_{\text{out}}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(p^{\mu}-{p^{\prime}}^{\mu})\delta_{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}\,S_{\ell}^{*}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s).

(131)

Plugging these to (
126
) we can schematically write the unitarity condition (
127
) as

\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{ab}&S_{\ell}^{\dagger}{}_{ab}\\
S_{\ell}{}_{ab}&\delta_{ab}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0.

(132)

Identical particles

In case either the incoming particles 1 and 2 or outgoing particles 3 and 4 are identical there exist relations (
117
) and (
118
) between the partial amplitudes. This introduces redundancies into the condition (
132
). In order to remove these redundancies we restrict our attention to states with
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}
(in case of identical incoming particles) and
\lambda_{3}\geq\lambda_{4}
(in case of identical outgoing particles).

Parity invariance

In the presence of parity invariance various partial amplitudes entering (
132
) are related according to (
122
). As a consequence the condition (
132
) again becomes redundant. One can reformulate the condition (
132
) in an equivalent but less redundant way by considering parity eigenstates. One then repeats the procedure above taking into account the fact that Parity invariance forbids transitions between parity even and parity odd states. As a result we get two separate positivity conditions for parity even and parity odd states

\mathcal{H}_{\ell}^{+}(s)\succeq 0,\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\ell}^{-}(s)\succeq 0.

(133)

We will see an explicit example of this in section
3.2
.

Time reversal invariance

Time reversal invariance relates the scattering amplitudes for the process
12\rightarrow 34
to the scattering amplitudes for the process
34\rightarrow 12
. Therefore in general time reversal does not have any implications for the matrix
\mathcal{H}_{\ell}
since all its elements are scattering amplitudes for the
12\rightarrow 34
. However in the special case of elastic scattering i.e
12\rightarrow 12
, time reversal invariance (
123
) implies that the sub-matrix
S_{ij}
is symmetric
23
23

23

Note that
|\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}|^{2}=1

S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(s)=S_{\ell}{}^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}_{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s)

(134)

2.7
Kinematic non-analyticities and constraints

This section is devoted to the study of the behaviour of COM interacting scattering amplitudes defined in (
69
) at some very particular values of the Mandelstam variables
s
,
t
and
u
. For simplicity we focus on the case of identical particles with mass
m
and spin
j
.

Using (
66
) the center of mass frame (
65
) can be written in the following way

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\text{com}}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2},0,0,+\sqrt{\frac{s}{4}-m^{2}}\right),

(135)

\displaystyle p_{2}^{\text{com}}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2},0,0,-\sqrt{\frac{s}{4}-m^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle p_{3}^{\text{com}}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2},+\frac{\sqrt{tu}}{\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}},0,+\frac{t-u}{2\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}}\right),

\displaystyle p_{4}^{\text{com}}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2},-\frac{\sqrt{tu}}{\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}},0,-\frac{t-u}{2\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}}\right).

The center of mass amplitude (
69
) is strictly defined in the physical domain of the Mandelstam variables (
68
). If one attempts however to analytically continue the COM frame amplitudes to arbitrary complex values of
s
,
t
and
u
, as can be already expected from (
135
), one will encounter non-analyticities (poles and branch points) at

s=4m^{2},\quad s=0,\quad t=0,\quad u=0.

(136)

Some of these non-analyticities have a purely kinematic nature and have nothing to do with the dynamics of the theory. Our goal here is to isolate them. In what follows we will formulate the problem of kinematic non-analyticities precisely and then discuss each of the special points (
136
) in detail. (A concrete example will be presented in section
3.1
.) For more details on the subject see chapter 7.3 in
Martin:102663
and references therein.

Recall the definition of helicity states (
12
), two particle center of mass states (
33
) and center of mass amplitudes (
69
). Using them we can write explicitly the 1PS describing the center of mass scattering process as

\displaystyle|m,+\vec{p}_{z};j,\lambda_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv e^{-i\eta K_{3}}|m,\vec{0};j,\lambda_{1}\rangle,

(137)

\displaystyle|m,-\vec{p}_{z};j,\lambda_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv e^{-i\pi J_{3}}e^{-i(\pi-0)J_{2}}e^{+i\pi J_{3}}e^{-i\eta K_{3}}|m,\vec{0};j,\lambda_{2}\rangle,

\displaystyle|m,+\vec{p}_{\theta};j,\lambda_{3}\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv e^{-i\theta J_{2}}e^{-i\eta K_{3}}|m,\vec{0};j,\lambda_{3}\rangle,

\displaystyle|m,-\vec{p}_{\theta};j,\lambda_{4}\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv e^{-i\pi J_{3}}e^{-i(\pi-\theta)J_{2}}e^{+i\pi J_{3}}e^{-i\eta K_{3}}|m,\vec{0};j,\lambda_{4}\rangle,

where
\vec{p}_{z}
is the 3-momentum in the positive
z
-direction,
\vec{p}_{\theta}
is the 3-momentum in the x-z plane,
J_{2}
is the generator of rotations around the
y
-axis,
K_{3}
is the boost in the
z
-direction. In the center of mass frame due to (
66
) the angle
\theta
and the rapidity
\eta
defined in (
13
) can be related to the Mandelstam variables as follows

\cos\theta=\frac{t-u}{s-4m^{2}},\quad\sin\theta=\frac{2\sqrt{tu}}{s-4m^{2}},\quad\sinh\eta=\frac{\sqrt{s-4m^{2}}}{2m},\quad\cosh\eta=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2m}.

(138)

The non-analyticities at
s=4m^{2}
,
s=0
,
t=0
and
u=0
of these expressions
enter the center of mass amplitude (
69
) via (
137
).

The phenomena of kinematic non-analyticities is closely related to the phenomena of kinematic constraints. When defining the COM scattering amplitudes (
69
) we have used up all of the Lorentz symmetry to bring the scattering particles to the x-z plane. However at the special points (
136
) we get an enhancement of symmetry. For instance at
s=4m^{2}
the system is
SO(3)
symmetric, at
s=0
the system is
SO(1,1)
symmetric and at
t=0
and
u=0
the system is
SO(2)
symmetric. This is straightforward to see from (
135
).
24
24

24

Notice that for the physical range of Mandelstam values
t=O(s-4m^{2})
and
u=O(s-4m^{2})
which means that
\cos\theta
and
\sin\theta
are finite. This is not the case anymore once we promote
s
and
t
to independent complex variables during the analytic continuation process.
