). The particles are identical and are therefore represented by the same colour.

In a generic situation, the two-particle states in the reducible and irreducible representations are related by (
327
). Since the Lorentz transformation property of the states are the same for identical and distinct particles, we can just use the decomposition formula (
327
) but with an unspecified normalization
\mathcal{N}_{id}
which we fix later in the section. One has

|c,\vec{0};{\ell},\lambda;\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}=\mathcal{N}_{id}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\int_{-1}^{+1}d\cos\theta e^{-i(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)\phi}d^{{\ell}}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(\theta)|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}.

(472)

We can use this formula to deduce the symmetry property of the state (
471
) under the exchange of two particles. Plugging (
467
) into the right-hand side of (
472
) we get

\displaystyle(-1)^{2j}\mathcal{N}_{id}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta\sin\theta\,e^{-i(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)\phi}d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(\theta)|(\boldsymbol{p},\pi-\theta,\phi+\pi);\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\rangle_{id}=

(473)

\displaystyle(-1)^{2j}\mathcal{N}_{id}\int_{\pi}^{3\pi}d\phi^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta^{\prime}\sin\theta^{\prime}e^{-i(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)(\phi^{\prime}-\pi)}d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(\pi-\theta^{\prime})|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime});\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\rangle_{id}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{2j}e^{i\pi(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)}(-1)^{{\ell}+{\lambda}}\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{id}\int_{\pi}^{3\pi}d\phi^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\pi}d\theta^{\prime}\sin\theta^{\prime}e^{-i(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)\phi^{\prime}}d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{21}}(\theta^{\prime})|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta^{\prime},\phi^{\prime});\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\rangle_{id}.

In the second line we changed the integration variables from
\theta
and
\phi
to
\theta^{\prime}\equiv\pi-\theta
and
\phi^{\prime}\equiv\phi+\pi
. In the third line we used (
414
) to rewrite the small Wigner d-matrix. Also recall the definition

\lambda_{12}\equiv\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}.

(474)

The last line in (
473
) simply contains the two-particle irrep (
472
). In order to see this, we refer the reader to (
416
) and the discussion below. Combining (
472
) and (
473
), and taking into account (
402
) we finally get
46
46

46

Notice that due to (
475
) the two-particle irreps with
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
exist only for even spins
{\ell}
. When
\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}
we can form two linear combinations from two-particle irreps, one of which exists only for even spins
{\ell}
and the other one only for odd
{\ell}
.

|c,\vec{0};{\ell},\lambda;\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}=(-1)^{{\ell}+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}|c,0;{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\rangle_{id}.

(475)

We can now define two-particle irreps (
471
) describing identical particles in terms of generic two-particle irreps by requiring that (
471
) automatically satisfies the condition (
475
). Our choice here is as follows

|c,0,{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(|c,0,{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle+(-1)^{\ell+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}|c,0,{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\rangle\right).

(476)

The normalization of the states (
476
) follows from the normalization of each of the two terms fixed by (
29
) together with (
32
). As a result the normalization of the states (
476
) is given by (
29
) along with

\delta_{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}+(-1)^{\ell+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}\delta_{\lambda_{2}\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\right).

(477)

Having defined (
476
), we can apply (
36
) to both terms in the right-hand side of (
465
).
Using the property of the small Wigner d-matrix (
414
) we get

|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,\phi);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{\text{id}}=\sqrt{2}\sum_{\ell,\lambda}C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})e^{i(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)\phi}d^{(\ell)}_{\lambda\lambda_{12}}(\theta)|c,0;{\ell},{\lambda};\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id},

(478)

where the coefficient
C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})
is given by (
39
).
As before we can invert the above equation to get precisely (
472
) with

\mathcal{N}_{id}=\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi\sqrt{2}C_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{p})}.

(479)

C.3
Constraints on scattering amplitudes

Consider now the scattering of identical particles.
47
47

47

More generally one can consider scattering processes with only incoming or outgoing particles being identical. As presented, most of the results in this section still apply to these situations.
The amplitude describing such a situation is defined as

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)\times(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(0)\equiv{}_{id}\langle(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\theta,0);\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}|T|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id},

(480)

where the identical two-particle states were defined in (
465
). We now deduce constraints the amplitude (
480
) must obey in order to incorporate the symmetry property (
467
). In practice we will use (
467
) rewritten in the form (
469
).

Let us start with the outgoing particles 3 and 4. Using (
469
) one can rewrite the right-hand side of (
480
) as

\displaystyle{}_{id}\langle(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\theta,0);

\displaystyle\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}|T|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}

(481)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}{}_{id}\langle(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\pi-\theta,0);\lambda_{4},\lambda_{3}|e^{i\pi J_{3}}T|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}{}_{id}\langle(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\pi-\theta,0);\lambda_{4},\lambda_{3}|e^{i\pi J_{3}}Te^{-i\pi J_{3}}e^{i\pi J_{3}}|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{}_{id}\langle(\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\pi-\theta,0);\lambda_{4},\lambda_{3}|T|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}.

In the third line we simply injected the identity operator made out of
z
-rotations. In the fourth line we used the fact that the states with the momentum along the z-axis are eigenstates of
J_{3}
generators. Looking at the definition of the Mandelstam variables (
504
) and (
505
) we see that the exchange
\theta\leftrightarrow\pi-\theta
simply corresponds to
t\leftrightarrow u
. Combining (
481
) with (
480
) we obtain

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4}}T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{3}}(s,u,t),

(482)

which is nothing but the (34)
t-u
crossing equation.

Let now address the incoming particles 1 and 2. First, we need the following relation which holds true for generic two-particle states (and therefore also for identical two-particle states)

|(\boldsymbol{p},\theta,0);\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}\rangle=(-1)^{\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}e^{-i\pi J_{3}}e^{-i\pi J_{2}}|(\boldsymbol{p},\pi-\theta,0);\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}\rangle.

(483)

It simply follows from (
320
) and (
297
), see also (
255
) and (
304
). Due to (
469
) and (
320
) we also have the following relation for identical particles

\displaystyle|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\rangle_{id}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}e^{-i\pi J_{3}}e^{-i\pi J_{2}}|(\boldsymbol{p},0,0);\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}\rangle_{id}.

(484)

Using both (
483
) and (
483
) in the right-hand side of (
480
), and the fact that the scattering operator is invariant under rotations we conclude that

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}}T_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,u,t),

(485)

which is nothing but the (12)
t-u
crossing equation.

Combining both crossing equations (
482
) and (
485
) we obtain the following purely kinematic constraint

\displaystyle T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(s,t,u)

\displaystyle=T_{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{3}}(s,t,u).

(486)

Here we used the fact that
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{4}
is always an integer.

Appendix D
Center of mass frame

In this section we define the center of mass frame describing two-, three- and four-point amplitudes.

D.1
Two-point amplitudes

We start with the two-point amplitude defined in (
60
). It reads

{}_{free}\langle c_{1},\vec{p}_{1};{\ell}_{1},{\lambda}_{1};\gamma_{1}|T|c_{2},\vec{p}_{2};{\ell}_{2},{\lambda}_{2};\gamma_{2}\rangle_{free}.

(487)

It is non-zero only for
\vec{p}_{1}=\vec{p}_{2}
due to translation symmetry. By using the three boost generators one can set

\vec{p}_{1}=\vec{p}_{2}=0.

(488)

The remaining symmetry is
SO(3)
. The matrix element (
487
) at the point (
488
) must be invariant under this
SO(3)
symmetry. Applying an
SO(3)
rotation to (
487
) we get

{}_{free}\langle c_{1},\vec{0};{\ell}_{1},{\lambda}_{1};\gamma_{1}|T|c_{2},\vec{0};{\ell}_{2},{\lambda}_{2};\gamma_{2}\rangle_{free}=\\
\sum_{\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}{}_{free}\langle c_{1},\vec{0};{\ell}_{1},{\lambda}_{1}^{\prime};\gamma_{1}|T|c_{2},\vec{0};{\ell}_{2},{\lambda}_{2}^{\prime};\gamma_{2}\rangle_{free}\times\mathscr{D}^{*({\ell}_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(\vec{\omega_{1}})\mathscr{D}^{({\ell}_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(\vec{\omega_{2}}).

(489)

In order to make the amplitude invariant one has to demand that

{}_{free}\langle c_{1},\vec{0};{\ell}_{1},{\lambda}_{1};\gamma_{1}|T|c_{2},\vec{0};{\ell}_{2},{\lambda}_{2};\gamma_{2}\rangle_{free}\propto\delta_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\delta_{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}}.

(490)

Then due to the orthogonality (
265
) the Wigner D-matrices disappear completely and one recovers the invariance of the two-point amplitude. The condition (
490
) means that there is only one independent two-point amplitude

N_{2}=1.

(491)

D.2
Three-point amplitudes

Consider the following matrix element

{}_{out}\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle_{in}={}_{free}\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}|S|c,\vec{p};{\ell},{\lambda};\gamma\rangle_{free}.

(492)

It appears in the discussion of poles, see for example section 2.5.1 in
Karateev:2019ymz
. It also appears in computations of partial amplitudes, see the end of appendix
H
. It is interesting to ask what happens if the ket state in (
492
) is actually a one particle state, namely when we deal with the following object

{}_{out}\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}|\kappa_{3}\rangle_{in}={}_{free}\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}|S|\kappa_{3}\rangle_{free}.

(493)

It describes the decay process of the asymptotic state 3 into two asymptotic states 1 and 2. Strictly speaking such a matrix element must be zero, since asymptotic states by definition cannot decay.
48
48

48

If a particle is unstable an observer after waiting long enough will see the decay product which is described by true asymptotic states.
In some circumstances when a particle is unstable but lives long enough it might be useful however to treat it as an approximate asymptotic state and prescribe physical meaning to (
493
).

Let us discuss the spin structure of (
493
) in the COM frame.
By using the three boosts we can set
\vec{p}_{3}=0
. By using two rotations we can move to the following final frame

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\{E_{1},\;0,\;0,\;+\boldsymbol{p}\},

(494)

\displaystyle p_{2}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\{E_{2},\;0,\;0,\;-\boldsymbol{p}\},

\displaystyle p_{3}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\{E_{3},\;0,\;0,\;0\},

where the energies read as

E_{1}=\frac{\big|m_{3}^{2}+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\big|}{2\,m_{3}},\quad E_{2}=\frac{\big|m_{3}^{2}-m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}\big|}{2\,m_{3}},\quad E_{3}=m_{3}

(495)

and we have

\boldsymbol{p}=\frac{1}{2m_{3}}\;\sqrt{(m_{3}+m_{1}+m_{2})(m_{3}-m_{1}-m_{2})(m_{3}-m_{1}+m_{2})(m_{3}+m_{1}-m_{2})}.

(496)

The value
\boldsymbol{p}
should be real, this enforces the condition

m_{3}\geq m_{1}+m_{2}.

(497)

Having chosen the frame (
494
) one is left with a single generator of rotations around the z-axis. This means that one has a remaining
SO(2)
symmetry and the matrix element (
493
) in the frame (
494
) must be invariant under it. To find the consequence of this symmetry one can
inject the identity operator composed out of z-rotations into (
493
) twice and requiring that the matrix element remains invariant. It leads to the following constraint

\lambda_{3}=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}.

(498)

Given the condition (
498
) one can easily count the number of different three-point amplitudes
N_{3}
. It is given by

N_{3}=\sum_{\lambda_{1}=-j_{1}}^{+j_{1}}\sum_{\lambda_{2}=-j_{2}}^{+j_{2}}\sum_{\lambda_{3}=-j_{3}}^{+j_{3}}\delta_{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}.

(499)

This expression was rewritten in a compact form in
Kravchuk:2016qvl
, it reads as

N_{3}=(2j_{1}+1)(2j_{2}+1)-r(r+1),\quad r\equiv\max(j_{1}+j_{2}-j_{3},\,0),\quad j_{1}\leq j_{2}\leq j_{3}.

(500)

D.3
Four-point amplitudes

The four-point amplitude was defined in (
48
). Using all the generators of the Lorentz group, we can bring this amplitude to the following frame

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\{E_{1},0,0,+\boldsymbol{p}\},

(501)

\displaystyle p_{2}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\{E_{2},0,0,-\boldsymbol{p}\},

\displaystyle p_{3}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\{E_{3},+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta\},

\displaystyle p_{4}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\{E_{4},-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta\},

where
\boldsymbol{p}\geq 0
,
\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\geq 0
and
\theta\in[0,\pi]
and the energies are given by

E_{1}=\sqrt{m_{1}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{2}},\quad E_{2}=\sqrt{m_{2}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{2}},\quad E_{3}=\sqrt{m_{3}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime 2}},\quad E_{4}=\sqrt{m_{4}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime 2}}.

(502)

The Mandelstam variables in the COM frame (
501
) then read as

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=(E_{1}+E_{2})^{2}=(E_{3}+E_{4})^{2},

(503)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=m_{1}^{2}+m_{3}^{2}-2E_{1}E_{3}+2\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta,

(504)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=m_{1}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}-2E_{1}E_{4}-2\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta.

(505)

In the case of four-point COM amplitudes there is no additional symmetry left. Thus, the total number of amplitudes is obtained by simply counting all possible helicity configurations

N_{4}=(2\ell_{1}+1)(2\ell_{2}+1)(2\ell_{3}+1)(2\ell_{4}+1).

(506)

The relations (
503
) - (
505
) express the Mandelstam variables
(s,t,u)
in terms of
(\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\theta)
. We can also invert these relations as follows. From (
503
) we get

\boldsymbol{p}=\frac{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)}{2\sqrt{s}},\qquad\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}=\frac{\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)}{2\sqrt{s}},

(507)

where we have defined

\mathcal{L}_{ij}(s)\equiv\sqrt{\big(s-(m_{i}-m_{j})^{2}\big)\big(s-(m_{i}+m_{j})^{2}\big)}.

(508)

Plugging (
507
) into (
502
) we get the energies

\displaystyle E_{1}=\frac{s+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}},\;\;E_{2}=\frac{s-m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}},\;\;E_{3}=\frac{s+m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}},\;\;E_{4}=\frac{s-m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}}.

(509)

Subtracting (
505
) from (
504
) and using (
507
) and (
509
) we get

\displaystyle\cos\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{s(t-u)+(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})(m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})}{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)}.

(510)

In the range
\theta\in[0,\pi]
we can also write unambiguously

\sin\theta=\sqrt{1-\cos^{2}\theta}=\frac{2\sqrt{s}\,\sqrt{\Phi}}{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)},

(511)

where we have defined

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\equiv stu-s(m_{2}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})(m_{1}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})-t\,(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})(m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})+\Delta_{t}\,(m_{1}^{2}m_{4}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}m_{3}^{2})

\displaystyle=stu-s(m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})(m_{1}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})+u(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})(m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})+\Delta_{u}(m_{1}^{2}m_{3}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}m_{4}^{2})

(512)

together with

\Delta_{t}\equiv-m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}+m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2},\qquad\Delta_{u}\equiv-m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}.

(513)

Let us study the physical ranges of the Mandelstam variables. From (
502
) and (
503
) the following inequalities follow

s\geq\max\Big((m_{1}+m_{2})^{2},\,(m_{3}+m_{4})^{2}\Big).

(514)

Notice, that due to (
514
) all the energies
E_{i}
in (
509
) are positive and
\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)
,
\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)
are real as they should be.
Since the value of
\cos\theta
is bounded to be in the
[-1,+1]
interval, we can derive the following constraints on the values of the variable
t
from (
510
)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\in[v+\frac{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)}{2s},v-\frac{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)}{2s}],

(515)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\,(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}+m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}-s)-\frac{(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})(m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})}{2s}.

From (
514
) and (
515
) in the equal mass case we recover the familiar result

s\in[4m^{2},\,\infty),\quad t\in[-(s-4m^{2}),\,0].

(516)

There is a very special situation when

\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)=0\quad{\text{\bf or}}\quad\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)=0.

(517)

From (
507
) we see that this corresponds to when either incoming or outgoing particles are at rest. In such a situation we cannot define the angle between incoming and outgoing particles which can be see from (
510
) which is singular at (
517
). The values of
s
which lead to (
517
) are

s=(m_{1}\pm m_{2})^{2},\quad s=(m_{3}\pm m_{4})^{2}\,.

(518)

Appendix E
Crossing equations

The goal of this appendix is to prove the crossing relations in a general frame (
78
) - (
81
) and then derive the crossing equations in the COM frame (
82
) and (
84
) together with the Wigner angles
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{i}
.

E.1
Analytic continuation of four-momenta

Consider the interacting part of the scattering amplitude

T_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1}^{\mu_{1}},p_{2}^{\mu_{2}},p_{3}^{\mu_{3}},p_{4}^{\mu_{4}}).

(519)

This function is defined only for non-negative energies
p_{i}^{0}\geq 0
, provided that the following constraint is satisfied

p_{i}^{2}=-m^{2}_{i},

(520)

where
m_{i}\geq 0
are the masses of particles.
However crossing requires us to evaluate the amplitude (
519
) at
-p_{i}^{\mu}
points which means that one must extend the definition of (
519
) also to negative values of energies
p_{i}^{0}
.
This can be done by analytically continuing the amplitude (
519
) in each component of four 4-vectors
p_{i}^{\mu}
to the full complex plane while keeping the constraints (
520
) satisfied.
49
49

49

In other words by using the analytic continuation, the amplitude (
519
) can be defined as a function of
4\times 4=16
complex variables which satisfy the four constraints (
520
).
To perform this analytic continuation, one needs to choose the path in (complexified) momentum space.

To continue the discussion in more detail let us focus for simplicity on a function of a single 4-momentum

f(p^{\mu})

(521)

defined for
p^{0}\geq 0
and
p^{2}=-m^{2}
with the non-negative mass
m
. The case of four 4-momenta (
519
) follows straightforwardly by the repeated use of the steps here for each
i=1,2,3,4
. Using the spherical coordinates
\boldsymbol{p}
,
\theta
and
\phi
one can write

p^{0}=\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}+m^{2}},\qquad\begin{cases}p^{1}=\boldsymbol{p}\sin\theta\cos\phi,\\
p^{2}=\boldsymbol{p}\sin\theta\sin\phi,\\
p^{3}=\boldsymbol{p}\cos\theta,\end{cases}

(522)

where
\boldsymbol{p}\geq 0
is the length of the 3-vector
\vec{p}
. The relation
p^{2}=-m^{2}
can be rewritten as

\boldsymbol{p}=i\sqrt{(m-p^{0})(p^{0}+m)}.

(523)

If we promote
p^{0}
to the full complex plane, the function (
523
) will have an analytic structure as depicted on figure
11
with two branch points
\pm m
and two branch cuts ending in these points.

In order to study crossing we need to defined (
521
) at the following point

f(-p^{\mu}).

(524)

This can be achieved by performing the following analytic continuation

p^{0}+i\epsilon\rightarrow\text{complex value}\rightarrow-p^{0}-i\epsilon,\quad\vec{p}\rightarrow\text{complex value}\rightarrow-\vec{p}.

(525)

In the
p^{0}
complex plane two different options for such an analytic continuation are depicted in figure
11

50
50

50

To be precise, the two different paths for analytic continuation take us to two different points on the Riemann surface.
. Note that the original domain of physical energy
p^{0}\geq m
by convention lies slightly above the right-hand branch cut.

p^{0}
m
-m
A.C. 1
A.C. 2
A
B

Figure 11:
The complex
p^{0}
plane. We depict the analytic structure of the function
\boldsymbol{p}(p^{0})
defined in (
523
). It has two branch cuts. The original domain of the function
\boldsymbol{p}
is given by positive values
p^{0}
slightly above the right cut. We define
\boldsymbol{p}
for negative values of
p^{0}
by an analytic continuation. The two different options are depicted in red and blue. Two paths together encircle the
+m
branch point and thus differ by a monodromy around that point.

Now let us investigate the behavior of the function (
523
) depending on the chosen path of the analytic continuation. If the path does not cross any branch cuts we simply get

\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow\text{complex value}\rightarrow+\boldsymbol{p}.

(526)

In case we cross once one of the branch cuts we get an extra phase due to the monodromy around the associated branch point which leads to

p^{0}\pm m\rightarrow(p^{0}\pm m)e^{2\pi i}.

As a result for this path we get the following

\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow\text{complex value}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{p}.

(527)

To summarize, the analytic continuation (
525
) can be implemented in two different ways depending on the path chosen on figure
11
. The two distinct options (
526
) and (
527
) due to (
522
) read as

\displaystyle p^{0}\rightarrow-p^{0},\quad\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow+\boldsymbol{p},\quad\theta\rightarrow\pi-\theta,\quad\phi\rightarrow\pi+\phi,

(528)

\displaystyle p^{0}\rightarrow-p^{0},\quad\boldsymbol{p}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{p},\quad\theta\rightarrow\theta,\quad\quad\;\;\;\phi\rightarrow\phi.

(529)

The first option (
528
) is commonly used in the literature. The second option (
529
) is more suitable for massless particles since two branch cuts in figure
11
unite and one cannot choose a path for the analytic continuation without crossing any of the two branch cuts. For treating massless particles one can also use the first option (
528
) and take the limit
m\rightarrow 0
at the very end.

The helicity states are defined via (
12
) and at a practical level depend only on the rapidity and two angles
(\eta,\theta,\phi)
. Using the definition of rapidity (
13
) we can write (
528
) and (
529
) as

\displaystyle\eta\rightarrow i\pi-\eta,\quad\theta\rightarrow\pi-\theta,\quad\phi\rightarrow\pi+\phi,

(530)

\displaystyle\eta\rightarrow i\pi+\eta,\quad\theta\rightarrow\theta,\quad\quad\;\;\;\phi\rightarrow\phi.

(531)

E.2
Crossing equations in a general frame

Given an amplitude of the
12\rightarrow 34
process in a generic frame we would like to relate it in this appendix to the four “crossed” amplitudes associated to the following processes

\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1},\qquad 1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4,\qquad\bar{3}2\rightarrow\bar{1}4,\qquad 1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}.

(532)

We refer to these relations as the crossing equations. To be concrete we will focus on writing the crossing equations for
12\rightarrow 34
and
\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}
amplitudes, the rest will follow by analogy.

Given an amplitude

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4}),

(533)

one can obtain the amplitude
\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}
by crossing particles 1 and 4. The ingoing particle 1 becomes then the outgoing one and the outgoing particle 4 becomes the ingoing one. As a result we need to make the following replacements:
p_{1}^{\mu}\rightarrow-p_{1}^{\mu}
and
p_{4}^{\mu}\rightarrow-p_{4}^{\mu}
. Moreover if a particle
1
has a charge (or more generally transforms in some representation of a global group) the particle
\bar{i}
has the opposite charge (transforms in the conjugate representation).
As a result (
533
) under crossing 1-4 becomes

T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1}).

(534)

In both (
533
) and (
534
) all 4-momenta have positive energies
p_{i}^{0}>0
and are on-shell (
55
).

Without using the LSZ reduction formula one can only postulate crossing. It states that one can define a single “mother amplitude” with complex values of 4-momenta such that all the amplitudes in (
533
) and (
534
) are its boundary values. This is known as the Mandelstam hypothesis. One cannot however simply equate (
533
) and (
534
). At the very least these amplitudes should have the same Lorentz transformation properties. We will use this requirement to fix the crossing equations up to an overall phase. This is the original way by which general spin crossing equations were derived in
Trueman:1964zza
.

Consider the transformation property of the amplitude describing the
12\rightarrow 34
process under generic Lorentz transformations
\Lambda
. According to (
62
) it reads as

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=

(535)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(p_{2},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(p_{3},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(p_{4},\Lambda)T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{4}^{\prime}}(p_{1}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}^{\prime},p_{4}^{\prime}).

Similarly the crossed amplitude describing the
\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}
process transforms as

\displaystyle T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1})=

(536)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(-p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(p_{2},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(p_{3},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(-p_{4},\Lambda)T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}(-p_{4}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}^{\prime},-p_{1}^{\prime}).

In the equations (
535
) and (
536
) we have schematically denoted the arguments of the Wigner D-matrices by
(p_{i},\Lambda)
. In practice they depend only on three Wigner angles which correspond to the
p_{i}\rightarrow\Lambda p_{i}
Lorentz transformation. We denote these angles as

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j_{i})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\lambda_{i}}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathscr{D}^{(j_{i})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\lambda_{i}}(+p,\Lambda),

(537)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j_{i})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{i}\lambda_{i}}(\bar{\alpha}_{i},\bar{\beta}_{i},\bar{\gamma}_{i})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda_{i}}(-p_{i},\Lambda),

(538)

where
i=1,2,3,4
. The Wigner angles
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i})
generically differ from
(\bar{\alpha}_{i},\bar{\beta}_{i},\bar{\gamma}_{i})
. They are however closely related. The main technical task of this appendix is to understand precisely how.

We would like to equate the amplitudes which transform in the same way under Lorentz transformations. Thus, in practice we need to compare the transformation properties of (
535
) and (
536
). This in turn boils down to comparing the Wigner D-matrices

\mathscr{D}^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\quad\text{vs.}\quad\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(-p_{1},\Lambda)\qquad\text{\bf and}\qquad\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(+p_{4},\Lambda)\quad\text{vs.}\quad\mathscr{D}^{(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(-p_{4},\Lambda).

In order to do this recall that according to (
308
) and (
310
) the Wigner rotation matrices are defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(+p,\Lambda):\quad R(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)

\displaystyle=U_{\text{h}}(+p^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda U_{\text{h}}(+p),

(539)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta},\bar{\gamma})=\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(-p,\Lambda):\quad R(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta},\bar{\gamma})

\displaystyle=U_{\text{h}}(-p^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda U_{\text{h}}(-p),

(540)

where the helicity transformation
U_{\text{h}}(+p)
is defined via (
304
). We repeat this definition here for convenience

\displaystyle U_{\text{h}}(+p)

\displaystyle=R(\phi,\theta,-\phi)\times B_{3}(\eta).

(541)

The quantity
U_{\text{h}}(-p)
is defined from
U_{\text{h}}(+p)
by analytic continuation. As discussed in the previous section there are two distinct ways to do it, using (
530
) or (
531
). Below we address the two options separately.

E.2.1
Analytic continuation 1

Using (
541
) and (
530
) we get

\displaystyle U_{\text{h}}(-p)

\displaystyle=R(\pi+\phi,\,\pi-\theta,\,-\pi-\phi)\times B_{3}(i\pi-\eta).

(542)

We can then focus on the vector representation defined in appendix
A.3
and compute the Wigner angles in (
539
) and (
540
) brute force using computer algebra.
51
51

51

Given a generic
3\times 3
rotation matrix it is straightforward to determine
\tan\alpha
,
\cos\beta
and
\tan\gamma
without any ambiguity. In order to determine the rest of trigonometric functions it is necessary to choose the region of
\beta
angle. One can either have
\beta\in[0,\pi]
or
\beta\in[0,-\pi]
.
,
52
52

52

By convention
\beta\in[0,\pi]
, however we are free to choose either
\bar{\beta}\in[0,\pi]
or
\bar{\beta}\in[0,-\pi]
. We make the latter choice, however both options lead to the same conclusion in the very end.
,
53
53

53

We first perform this computation for a generic infinitesimal Lorentz transformation. We then focus on some simple finite transformations like boosts along the
x
,
y
and
z
axes separately.

Comparing the results we conclude that

\bar{\alpha}=-\alpha+2\phi^{\prime},\quad\bar{\beta}=-\beta,\quad\bar{\gamma}=-\gamma-2\phi.

(543)

From the properties of the Wigner
D
-matrix it then follows that

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta},\bar{\gamma})

\displaystyle=e^{-2i\phi^{\prime}\lambda^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime},\lambda}(-\alpha,-\beta,-\gamma)e^{+2i\phi\lambda}

(544)

\displaystyle=e^{i(\pi-2\phi^{\prime})\lambda^{\prime}}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi)\lambda}\mathscr{D}^{*(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma),

see appendix A.2 of
Martin:102663
for the summary of properties of D matrices. In other words

\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(-p,\Lambda)=e^{i(\pi-2\phi^{\prime})\lambda^{\prime}}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi)\lambda}\mathscr{D}^{*(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(+p,\Lambda).

(545)

With the help of (
545
) one can rewrite the transformation property (
536
) in the following form
54
54

54

In writing this formula we assumed that the relation between
\mathscr{D}^{*(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(-p,\Lambda)
and
\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(+p,\Lambda)
is obtained from (
545
) by taking complex conjugation. This is not quite correct because we are interested in the analytic continuation along the same path in the
p^{0}
complex plane and not along the complex conjugated path. However, the two continuations are related by a monodromy around square root branch points that can only give rise to an overall helicity-independent phase which is irrelevant for the discussion below.

\displaystyle T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1})=\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi^{\prime}_{1})\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}e^{+i(\pi-2\phi_{1})\lambda_{1}}e^{i(\pi-2\phi^{\prime}_{4})\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi_{4})\lambda_{4}}

(546)

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(+p_{4},\Lambda)T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}(-p_{4}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}^{\prime},-p_{1}^{\prime}),,

which can be rewritten as

\Bigg(e^{-i(\pi-2\phi_{1})\lambda_{1}}e^{+i(\pi-2\phi_{4})\lambda_{4}}T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1})\Bigg)=\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\\
\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(+p_{4},\Lambda)\Bigg(e^{-i(\pi-2\phi^{\prime}_{1})\lambda^{\prime}_{1}}e^{i(\pi-2\phi^{\prime}_{4})\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}(-p_{4}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}^{\prime},-p_{1}^{\prime})\Bigg).

(547)

Comparing (
535
) and (
547
) we see that the following two objects transform in the same way under generic Lorentz transformations

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\;\;\text{vs.}\;\;e^{-i(\pi-2\phi_{1})\lambda_{1}}e^{i(\pi-2\phi_{4})\lambda_{4}}T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1}).

(548)

As a result, the only way to write crossing equations which involve objects transforming in the same way under generic Lorentz tranformation is to equate the objects in (
548
). This procedure leaves undetermined an overall helicity independent phase. The other crossing equations follow by simply re-labeling the indices.

Our final answer reads as

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{14}^{(1)}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi_{1})\lambda_{1}}e^{i(\pi-2\phi_{4})\lambda_{4}}T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1}),

(549)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{23}^{(1)}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi_{2})\lambda_{2}}e^{i(\pi-2\phi_{3})\lambda_{3}}T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4}),

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{13}^{(1)}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi_{1})\lambda_{1}}e^{i(\pi-2\phi_{3})\lambda_{3}}T_{\bar{3}2\rightarrow 1\bar{4}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4}}(-p_{3},p_{2},-p_{1},p_{4}),

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{24}^{(1)}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi_{2})\lambda_{2}}e^{i(\pi-2\phi_{4})\lambda_{4}}T_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2}}(p_{1},-p_{4},p_{3},-p_{2}),

where
\epsilon_{14}^{(1)}
,
\epsilon_{23}^{(1)}
,
\epsilon_{13}^{(1)}
and
\epsilon_{24}^{(1)}
are the helicity independent phases unfixed by this procedure.

E.2.2
Analytic continuation 2

Using (
541
) and (
531
) we get

U_{\text{h}}(-p)=R(\phi,\,\theta,\,-\phi)\times B_{3}(i\pi+\eta)=-U_{\text{h}}(+p)R(\pi,0,0).

(550)

Plugging it into (
540
) one gets

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta},\bar{\gamma})=\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(-p,\Lambda):\quad R(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta},\bar{\gamma})

\displaystyle=R^{-1}(\pi,0,0)\Big(U_{\text{h}}(+p^{\prime})^{-1}\Lambda U_{\text{h}}(+p)\Big)R(\pi,0,0)

\displaystyle=R^{-1}(\pi,0,0)R(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)R(\pi,0,0).

(551)

Using the properties of the small Wigner d-matrices we can write then

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\bar{\alpha},\bar{\beta},\bar{\gamma})=e^{+i\pi\lambda^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)e^{-i\pi\lambda}=\mathscr{D}^{*(j)}_{-\lambda^{\prime},-\lambda}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma).

(552)

In other words we get

\mathscr{D}^{(j)}_{\lambda^{\prime}\lambda}(-p,\Lambda)=\mathscr{D}^{*(j)}_{-\lambda^{\prime},-\lambda}(+p,\Lambda).

(553)

Using this we can write the transformation property (
536
) as
55
55

55

See footnote
54
.

\displaystyle T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1})=

(554)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(j_{1})}_{-\lambda^{\prime}_{1},-\lambda_{1}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(+p_{2},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(+p_{3},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{4})}_{-\lambda^{\prime}_{4},-\lambda_{4}}(+p_{4},\Lambda)T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{4}^{\prime},\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}(-p_{4}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}^{\prime},-p_{1}^{\prime}).

Let us rename
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{4}
and call them
-\lambda_{1}
and
-\lambda_{4}
. We can also do the same for
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}
and
\lambda_{4}^{\prime}
since they are dummy indices and the summation covers all the options. We get then

\displaystyle T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{-\lambda_{4},+\lambda_{2}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1})=

(555)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\lambda_{1}}(+p_{1},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(+p_{2},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(+p_{3},\Lambda)\mathscr{D}^{*(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4},\lambda_{4}}(+p_{4},\Lambda)T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{-\lambda_{4}^{\prime},+\lambda_{2}^{\prime}}^{+\lambda_{3}^{\prime},-\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}(-p_{4}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},p_{3}^{\prime},-p_{1}^{\prime}).

Comparing (
535
) and (
555
) we conclude that the following two objects transform in the same way under the generic Lorentz transformations

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(+p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},+p_{4})\quad\text{vs.}\quad T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{-\lambda_{4},+\lambda_{2}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1}).

(556)

Analogous discussion holds for other crossings we can thus write the following equations

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{14}^{(2)}\;T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{\;}{}_{-\lambda_{4},+\lambda_{2}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},+p_{2},+p_{3},-p_{1}),

(557)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{23}^{(2)}\;T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{\;}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},+\lambda_{4}}(+p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},+p_{4}),

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{13}^{(2)}\;T_{\bar{3}2\rightarrow 1\bar{4}}{\;}{}_{-\lambda_{3},+\lambda_{2}}^{-\lambda_{1},+\lambda_{4}}(-p_{3},+p_{2},-p_{1},+p_{4}),

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{24}^{(2)}\;T_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{\;}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{4}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{2}}(+p_{1},-p_{4},+p_{3},-p_{2}),

where as before
\epsilon_{14}^{(2)}
,
\epsilon_{23}^{(2)}
,
\epsilon_{13}^{(2)}
and
\epsilon_{24}^{(2)}
are the helicity independent phases unfixed by this procedure.

E.3
Crossing equations in a general frame: LSZ derivation

In this section we derive the crossing equation (
79
) from the LSZ reduction formula in the case of spin
1/2
fermion scattering. The latter is carefully discussed in section 41 of
Srednicki:2007qs
. The LSZ reduction formula in this case reads as

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

(558)

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{3}x_{3}}\,[\bar{u}_{\lambda_{3}}(p_{3})(-i\not{\partial}_{3}+m_{3})]_{\alpha_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,[\bar{u}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-i\not{\partial}_{4}+m_{4})]_{\alpha_{4}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad\langle\Omega|T\{\Psi_{\alpha_{4}}(x_{4})\Psi_{\alpha_{3}}(x_{3})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad[(i\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}}_{1}+m_{1})u_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})]_{\alpha_{1}}\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad[(i\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}}_{2}+m_{2})u_{\lambda_{2}}(p_{2})]_{\alpha_{2}}\,e^{ip_{2}x_{2}},

where
|\Omega\rangle
denotes the vacuum state and
\Psi_{i}(x)
are 4-component Majorana or Dirac fields with masses
m_{i}
. Analogously one can write

\displaystyle T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{3},p_{2},p_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

(559)

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{ip_{3}x_{3}}\,[\bar{v}_{\lambda_{3}}(p_{3})(-i\not{\partial}_{3}+m_{3})]_{\alpha_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,[\bar{u}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-i\not{\partial}_{4}+m_{4})]_{\alpha_{4}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad\langle\Omega|T\{\Psi_{\alpha_{4}}(x_{4})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{2}}(x_{2})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\Psi_{\alpha_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad[(i\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}}_{1}+m_{1})u_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})]_{\alpha_{1}}\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad[(i\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}}_{2}+m_{2})v_{\lambda_{2}}(p_{2})]_{\alpha_{2}}\,e^{-ip_{2}x_{2}}.

In the above equations all the momenta have positive energy, namely
p_{i}^{0}>0
. In order to relate the two processes (
558
) and (
559
) one can analytically continue the latter process in
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
to allow for negative energies. Assuming such an analytic continuation exists one gets

\displaystyle T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x_{1}\,d^{4}x_{2}\,d^{4}x_{3}\,d^{4}x_{4}

(560)

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{3}x_{3}}\,[\bar{v}_{\lambda_{3}}(-p_{3})(-i\not{\partial}_{3}+m_{3})]_{\alpha_{3}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad e^{-ip_{4}x_{4}}\,[\bar{u}_{\lambda_{4}}(p_{4})(-i\not{\partial}_{4}+m_{4})]_{\alpha_{4}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad\langle\Omega|T\{\Psi_{\alpha_{4}}(x_{4})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{2}}(x_{2})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\Psi_{\alpha_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle_{connected}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad[(i\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}}_{1}+m_{1})u_{\lambda_{1}}(p_{1})]_{\alpha_{1}}\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\quad[(i\overleftarrow{\not{\partial}}_{2}+m_{2})v_{\lambda_{2}}(-p_{2})]_{\alpha_{2}}\,e^{ip_{2}x_{2}}.

Notice also that the anticommutation of fermionic operators manifests itself in the following relation

\langle\Omega|T\{\Psi_{\alpha_{4}}(x_{4})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{2}}(x_{2})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\Psi_{\alpha_{3}}(x_{3})\}|\Omega\rangle=\\
-\langle\Omega|T\{\Psi_{\alpha_{4}}(x_{4})\Psi_{\alpha_{3}}(x_{3})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1})\overline{\Psi}_{\alpha_{2}}(x_{2})\}|\Omega\rangle.

(561)

According to appendix
E.1
there are two ways of choosing the analytic continuation when writing (
560
). Below we discuss both of them in order.

Analytic continuation 1

Using the analytic continuation (
528
) and the definition (
225
) it follows straightforwardly

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}(-p)

\displaystyle=e^{i\lambda(\pi+2\phi)}v_{\lambda}(p),\qquad\bar{u}_{\lambda}(-p)=e^{i\lambda(\pi-2\phi)}\bar{v}_{\lambda}(p),

(562)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}(-p)

\displaystyle=e^{i\lambda(\pi-2\phi)}u_{\lambda}(p),\qquad\bar{v}_{\lambda}(-p)=e^{i\lambda(\pi+2\phi)}\bar{u}_{\lambda}(p).

Plugging (
562
) and (
561
) into (
560
) and comparing the result with (
558
)
we arrive at the following crossing equation

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=-e^{-i\lambda_{2}(\pi-2\phi_{2})}e^{-i\lambda_{3}(\pi+2\phi_{3})}T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4}).

(563)

Analogously one derives the other three crossing equations. The complete summary of crossing equations reads

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=-e^{-i\lambda_{1}(\pi-2\phi_{1})}e^{-i\lambda_{4}(\pi+2\phi_{4})}T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{}_{\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1}),

(564)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=-e^{-i\lambda_{2}(\pi-2\phi_{2})}e^{-i\lambda_{3}(\pi+2\phi_{3})}T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4}),

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=-e^{-i\lambda_{1}(\pi-2\phi_{1})}e^{-i\lambda_{3}(\pi+2\phi_{3})}T_{\bar{3}2\rightarrow\bar{1}4}{}_{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4}}(-p_{3},p_{2},-p_{1},p_{4}),

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=-e^{-i\lambda_{2}(\pi-2\phi_{2})}e^{-i\lambda_{4}(\pi+2\phi_{4})}T_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2}}(p_{1},-p_{4},p_{3},-p_{2}).

We can now compare the above to (
549
) and deduce the undetermined phases
56
56

56

Here we use the fact that
e^{2i\pi\lambda}=-1
since
\lambda
is half-integer.

\epsilon^{(1)}_{14}=\epsilon^{(1)}_{23}=\epsilon^{(1)}_{13}=\epsilon^{(1)}_{24}=+1.

(565)

Analytic continuation 2

Using the analytic continuation (
529
) and the definition (
225
) it follows straightforwardly

\displaystyle u_{\lambda}(-p)=i\,v_{-\lambda}(p),\qquad\bar{u}_{\lambda}(-p)=i\,\bar{v}_{-\lambda}(p),

(566)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}(-p)=i\,u_{-\lambda}(p),\qquad\bar{v}_{\lambda}(-p)=i\,\bar{u}_{-\lambda}(p).

Plugging (
566
) and (
561
) into (
560
) and comparing the result with (
558
)
we arrive at the following crossing equation

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},+\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4}).

(567)

Analogously one derives the other three crossing equations. The complete summary of crossing equations reads

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=T_{\bar{4}2\rightarrow 3\bar{1}}{}_{-\lambda_{4},+\lambda_{2}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{1}}(-p_{4},p_{2},p_{3},-p_{1}),

(568)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},+\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},-p_{3},-p_{2},p_{4}),

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=T_{\bar{3}2\rightarrow\bar{1}4}{}_{-\lambda_{3},+\lambda_{2}}^{-\lambda_{1},+\lambda_{4}}(-p_{3},p_{2},-p_{1},p_{4}),

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=T_{1\bar{4}\rightarrow 3\bar{2}}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{4}}^{+\lambda_{3},-\lambda_{2}}(p_{1},-p_{4},p_{3},-p_{2}).

Comparing the above with (
557
), we see that all the previously undetermined phases are

\epsilon^{(2)}_{14}=\epsilon^{(2)}_{23}=\epsilon^{(2)}_{13}=\epsilon^{(2)}_{24}=+1.

(569)

Concluding remarks

We have derived in this appendix the crossing equations for generic spin
1/2
particles in a general frame using the LSZ reduction formula. These depend on the analytic continuation. For the analytic continuation (
528
) our crossing equations are given by (
564
). For the analytic continuation (
529
) our crossing equations are given by (
568
). All these formulas remain (almost) the same even if some of the particles have spin different from 1/2. This follows from the fact that the spin structures of a generic spin particle can be represented by products of
u
and
v
objects. The only change in the crossing equations comes in the overall sign since some particles can now commute instead.

E.4
Crossing equations in the center of mass frame

The goal of this appendix is to write the crossing equations (
549
) and (
557
) in the center of mass frame. We will see that both analytic continuations lead to the same center of mass equations. The crossing equations 1-4 and 2-3 are called the
s-t
equations. Since they carry identical information, we focus only on the 2-3 crossing equation. Instead the crossing equations 1-3 and 2-4 are called the
s-u
equations. Since they also carry the same information we focus only the 2-4 crossing equation. The discussion of the
s-u
center of mass equations is identical to the
s-t
one, we will therefore only provide the final results without any intermediate steps.

E.4.1
s-t
crossing equation

According to appendix
E.2
the 2-3 crossing equation depending on the analytic continuation can take either of the two forms

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{23}^{(1)}e^{-i(\pi-2\phi_{2})\lambda_{2}}e^{i(\pi-2\phi_{3})\lambda_{3}}T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{3}}^{\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4}}(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4}),

(570)

\displaystyle T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{23}^{(2)}\;T_{1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4}{\;}{}_{+\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{3}}^{-\lambda_{2},+\lambda_{4}}(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4}),

(571)

where we have defined

P_{1}\equiv p_{1},\quad P_{2}\equiv-p_{3},\quad P_{3}\equiv-p_{2},\quad P_{4}\equiv p_{4}.

(572)

In the right hand-side of (
570
) and (
571
)
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
describe the incoming particles 1 and
\bar{3}
respectively, whereas
P_{3}
and
P_{4}
describe the outgoing particles
\bar{2}
and 4 respectively. The Mandelstam variables associated to the left-hand side are as usual

s\equiv-(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2},\quad t\equiv-(p_{1}-p_{3})^{2},\quad u\equiv-(p_{1}-p_{4})^{2}.

(573)

Instead the Mandelstam variables associated to the right-hand side are

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\equiv-(P_{1}+P_{2})^{2}=-(p_{1}-p_{3})^{2}=t,

(574)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\equiv-(P_{1}-P_{3})^{2}=-(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=s,

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\equiv-(P_{1}-P_{4})^{2}=-(p_{1}-p_{4})^{2}=u.

The Mandelstam variables remain invariant by definition under any Lorentz transformation.

Let us now evaluate the left-hand side of (
570
) and (
571
) in the center of mass frame of the
12\rightarrow 34
process denoted by
p^{\text{com}}_{i}
. It is defined in (
65
), we write it here again for convenience

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\text{com}}\equiv(E_{1},0,0,+\boldsymbol{p}),

(575)

\displaystyle p_{3}^{\text{com}}\equiv(E_{3},+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta),

\displaystyle p_{2}^{\text{com}}\equiv(E_{2},0,0,-\boldsymbol{p}),

\displaystyle p_{4}^{\text{com}}\equiv(E_{4},-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta).

The main feature of this frame is that it respects the center of mass conditions

\displaystyle p_{1}^{\text{com}}+p_{2}^{\text{com}}=(E_{1}+E_{2},0,0,0),

(576)

\displaystyle p_{3}^{\text{com}}+p_{4}^{\text{com}}=(E_{3}+E_{4},0,0,0).

Then the right-hand side of (
570
) and (
571
) will depend on

\displaystyle P_{1}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle\equiv(E_{1},0,0,\boldsymbol{p}),

(577)

\displaystyle P_{2}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle\equiv(-E_{3},-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta),

\displaystyle P_{3}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle\equiv(-E_{2},0,0,\boldsymbol{p}),

\displaystyle P_{4}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle\equiv(E_{4},-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta).

The latter obey

\displaystyle P_{1}^{\text{com}}-P_{3}^{\text{com}}=(E_{1}+E_{2},0,0,0),

(578)

\displaystyle P_{4}^{\text{com}}-P_{2}^{\text{com}}=(E_{3}+E_{4},0,0,0).

However, this is not the standard COM frame of the process
12\rightarrow 34
since it differs from (
576
). We refer to (
577
) as the (23) crossed COM frame.

Once the left-hand sides of (
570
) and (
571
) are evaluated in the
12\rightarrow 34
COM frame which respects (
576
) we do not get closed expressions since the right-hand side is not in the
1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4
COM frame but rather in the (23) crossed COM frame
57
57

57

Recall that we defined the amplitudes as functions of Mandelstam invariants in the COM frame.
. We therefore need an additional Lorentz transformation.
It turns out to be simpler to bring the left-hand side to the (23) crossed COM frame. This way upon (23) crossing we end up in the COM frame for the
1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4
. This is illustrated in figure
12
.

s channel COM frame
1

(i) Lorentz (complex)

(ii) Cross
2\leftrightarrow 3

Figure 12:
Schematic picture of (23) crossing for COM frame amplitudes.

We now consider the Lorentz transformation
\Lambda
such that

\begin{aligned} &p_{1}^{\text{com}}=(E_{1},0,0,+\boldsymbol{p})\\
&p_{2}^{\text{com}}=(E_{2},0,0,-\boldsymbol{p})\\
&p_{3}^{\text{com}}=(E_{3},+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta)\\
&p_{4}^{\text{com}}=(E_{4},-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta,0,-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta)\end{aligned}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\begin{aligned} &\hat{p}_{1}^{\text{com}}=(\hat{E}_{1},0,0,+\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}),\\
&\hat{p}_{2}^{\text{com}}=(\hat{E}_{2},-\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}\sin\hat{\theta},0,-\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}\cos\hat{\theta}),\\
&\hat{p}_{3}^{\text{com}}=(\hat{E}_{3},0,0,\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}),\\
&\hat{p}_{4}^{\text{com}}=(\hat{E}_{4},-\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}\sin\hat{\theta},0,-\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}\cos\hat{\theta}),\end{aligned}

(579)

where
\hat{E}_{i}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}
,
,\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}
and
\hat{\theta}
are the components of the 4-momenta in the new frame. The frame described by the right-hand side (
579
) is precisely the (23) crossed frame (
577
) since

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{1}^{\text{com}}-\hat{p}_{3}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle=(\hat{E}_{1}-\hat{E}_{3},0,0,0),

(580)

\displaystyle\hat{p}_{4}^{\text{com}}-\hat{p}_{2}^{\text{com}}

\displaystyle=(\hat{E}_{4}-\hat{E}_{2},0,0,0).

Due to the results of appendix
D.3
we have

\displaystyle E_{1}=\frac{s+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}},\;\;E_{2}=\frac{s-m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}}

\displaystyle,\;\ E_{3}=\frac{s+m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}},\;\;E_{4}=\frac{s-m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}},

(581)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{p}=\frac{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)}{2\sqrt{s}}

\displaystyle,\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}=\frac{\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)}{2\sqrt{s}}.

(582)

These are originally defined in the physical range of parameters of the
12\rightarrow 34
process, where in the case of identical particles we have

12\rightarrow 34:\qquad s\geq 4m^{2},\quad t\in[4m^{2}-s\;,0].

(583)

Remember that
s
is regularized as
s+i\epsilon
, where
\epsilon>0
. We can unambiguously continue all the formulas valid in the domain (
583
) to the physical domain of the
1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4
process which for identical particles read as

1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4:\qquad t\geq 4m^{2},\quad s\in[4m^{2}-t\;,0].

(584)

The Lorentz transformation which allows for (
579
) and (
580
) has the following form

\Lambda=R_{2}(\psi_{2})B_{3}(\chi)R_{2}(\psi_{1}),

(585)

where
R_{2}
and
B_{3}
are finite rotations around the y-axis and boost in the z-direction. Their form in the vector representation is given in appendix
A.3
. By requiring (
580
) we can determine the unknown parameters
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
and
\chi
.
First, using the rotation
R_{2}(\psi_{1})
we make the x-components of
p^{\text{com}}_{1}
and
p^{\text{com}}_{3}
equal. This is achieved for example with

\cos\psi_{1}=\frac{\boldsymbol{p}-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta}{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime 2}-2\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta}},\qquad\sin\psi_{1}=\frac{\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\sin\theta}{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime 2}-2\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta}}.

(586)

Second, using the boost
B_{3}(\chi)
along the z-axis we make the z-components of the rotated vectors
p^{\text{com}}_{1}
and
p^{\text{com}}_{3}
equal. This is achieved with

\displaystyle\cosh\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{E_{1}-E_{3}}{\sqrt{(E_{1}-E_{3})^{2}-\boldsymbol{p}^{2}-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime 2}+2\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta}}=\frac{m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2}}{2\sqrt{s}\sqrt{t}},

(587)

\displaystyle\sinh\chi

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sqrt{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}+\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime 2}-2\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta}}{\sqrt{(E_{1}-E_{3})^{2}-\boldsymbol{p}^{2}-\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime 2}+2\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cos\theta}}=-\frac{\sqrt{s^{-1}(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+m_{4}^{2})^{2}-4t}}{2\sqrt{t}}.

Third, the rotation
R_{2}(\psi_{2})
brings us to the desired frame. However, we will not need the explicit expression for the angle
\psi_{2}
. As a result we get the following expressions

\displaystyle\hat{E}_{1}=\frac{m_{1}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}+t}{2\sqrt{t}},\;\;\hat{E}_{2}=\frac{m_{4}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}-t}{2\sqrt{t}}

\displaystyle,\;\;\hat{E}_{3}=\frac{m_{1}^{2}-m_{3}^{2}-t}{2\sqrt{t}},\;\;\hat{E}_{4}=\frac{m_{4}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}+t}{2\sqrt{t}},

(588)

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{2}=\frac{(\mathcal{L}_{13}(t))^{2}}{4t}

\displaystyle,\;\;\;\;\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime\,2}=\frac{(\mathcal{L}_{24}(t))^{2}}{4t}.

(589)

There were several choices of signs in (
587
). We picked one such that the energy
\hat{E}_{1}
in (
588
) is non-negative in the physical domain (
584
) of the process
1\bar{3}\rightarrow\bar{2}4
.

Assuming that we have determined the correct values of parameters
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
and
\chi
, we can then straightforwardly compute the Wigner angles using the results of appendix
A.4
, which read

\cos\alpha_{i}=\frac{E_{i}\,\hat{E}_{i}-m_{i}^{2}\cosh\chi}{\boldsymbol{p}_{i}\,\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}},\quad\sin\alpha_{i}=\frac{m_{i}\sinh\chi}{\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}\,\sin(\theta_{i}+\psi_{1}).

(590)

Here the Wigner angles with the subscript
i=1,2,3,4
correspond to Lorentz transformations of
p_{i}^{\text{com}}
in (
579
). The spherical angles of the four-particles in (
579
) before the Lorentz transformation read as

\boldsymbol{p}_{1}=\boldsymbol{p}_{2}=\boldsymbol{p},\quad\theta_{1}=0,\quad\theta_{2}=\pi,\quad\boldsymbol{p}_{3}=\boldsymbol{p}_{4}=\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime},\quad\theta_{3}=\theta,\quad\theta_{4}=\theta+\pi.

(591)

Using the transformation property (
62
) of scattering amplitudes we can then write

T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}}(p_{1}^{\text{com}},p_{2}^{\text{com}},p_{3}^{\text{com}},p_{4}^{\text{com}})=\\
\sum_{\lambda_{i}^{\prime}}d^{(j_{1})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\lambda_{1}}(\alpha_{1})d^{(j_{2})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\lambda_{2}}(\alpha_{2})d^{(j_{3})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{3}\lambda_{3}}(\alpha_{3})d^{(j_{4})}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{4}\lambda_{4}}(\alpha_{4})T_{12\rightarrow 34}{\;}{}_{\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\lambda^{\prime}_{2}}^{\lambda^{\prime}_{3},\lambda^{\prime}_{4}}(\hat{p}_{1}^{\text{com}},\hat{p}_{3}^{\text{com}},\hat{p}_{2}^{\text{com}},\hat{p}_{4}^{\text{com}}).

(592)

Analytic continuation 1

Having performed the Lorentz transformations (
579
) we ended up with (
588
). These expressions contain an ambiguity on how to take a square root. This is related to the choice of the analytic continuation discussed in section
E
.
For the choice (
528
) in the domain (
584
) we have

\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}=+\frac{\mathcal{L}_{13}(t)}{2\sqrt{t}},\qquad\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}=+\frac{\mathcal{L}_{24}(t)}{2\sqrt{t}}.

(593)

The spherical angles of the four-momenta
\hat{p}_{i}^{\text{com}}
after the Lorentz transformation read as

\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}=\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}_{3}=\hat{\boldsymbol{p}},\quad\hat{\theta}_{1}=\hat{\theta}_{3}=0,\quad\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}_{2}=\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}_{4}=\hat{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime},\quad\hat{\theta}_{2}=\hat{\theta}_{4}=\hat{\theta}+\pi.

(594)

Using these, the boost (
587
) and (
590
) we obtain in full generality the cosines
58
58

58

The values computed here match precisely the ones of formula (13) in
Hara:1971kj
. Notice that our process
12\rightarrow 34
corresponds to their
ac\rightarrow bd
. Thus, in order to see the equivalence one needs to identify the labels as
1=a
,
2=c
,
3=b
and
4=d
.

\displaystyle\cos\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{+(s+m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})(t+m_{1}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})+2m_{1}^{2}\,\Delta_{t}}{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)\mathcal{L}_{13}(t)},

(595)

\displaystyle\cos\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(s-m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2})(t+m_{2}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})+2m_{2}^{2}\,\Delta_{t}}{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)\mathcal{L}_{24}(t)},

\displaystyle\cos\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{-(s+m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2})(t-m_{1}^{2}+m_{3}^{2})+2m_{3}^{2}\,\Delta_{t}}{\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)\mathcal{L}_{13}(t)},

\displaystyle\cos\alpha_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{+(s+m_{4}^{2}-m_{3}^{2})(t+m_{4}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})+2m_{4}^{2}\,\Delta_{t}}{\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)\mathcal{L}_{24}(t)},

together with sines

\displaystyle\sin\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2m_{1}\,\sqrt{\Phi}}{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)\mathcal{L}_{13}(t)},\quad\sin\alpha_{2}=+\frac{2m_{2}\,\sqrt{\Phi}}{\mathcal{L}_{12}(s)\mathcal{L}_{24}(t)},

(596)

\displaystyle\sin\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2m_{3}\,\sqrt{\Phi}}{\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)\mathcal{L}_{13}(t)},\quad\sin\alpha_{4}=+\frac{2m_{4}\,\sqrt{\Phi}}{\mathcal{L}_{34}(s)\mathcal{L}_{24}(t)},

where the objects
\Phi

were defined (

) and (

).

Using the crossing relation (

), where we set
\phi_{2}=\pi
and
